## Phase 2 — Fullscreen-pass infrastructure (`MOD-200`–`MOD-233`)

→ CNAEXT.md §5.2. Everything the four concrete passes share: the context struct, the abstract base,
a fullscreen-triangle drawer, a ping-pong target pool, and the `ShaderEffect` gaps found in `MOD-16`.

### 2.1 Core types

| ID | Task | Status | Acceptance criterion |
|---|---|---|---|
| MOD-200 | `PostProcessContext` struct (`source`, `sourceDepth`, `sourceNormals`, `destination`, `width`, `height`, `settings`, `elapsedSeconds`) | ✅ | Done. `PostProcessContext` carries source/depth/normals/destination, size, settings, elapsed time and the camera block. One struct rather than a signature per pass, so a chain of mixed passes is expressible. |
| MOD-201 | `detail::FullscreenTriangle` — one oversized triangle via `DrawUserPrimitives`, no VB (→ MOD-19) | ✅ | Done as `FullscreenPass` (see MOD-19 for the shape it took). |
| MOD-202 | `PostProcessPass` abstract base — `apply(ctx)`, `getName()`, `isSupported(device)` | ✅ | Done. `PostProcessPass` with `apply(ctx)`, `getName()` and `isSupported(device)`; the default `isSupported` answers the question every shader pass shares (`CustomEffects`). |
| MOD-203 | `PostProcessPass` common helper: bind destination (null = backbuffer), set viewport, restore previous target on scope exit | ✅ | `CNA::Graphics::ScopedRenderTarget` (`modules/graphics-ext/{include,src}/CNA/Graphics/ScopedRenderTarget.{hpp,cpp}`), used by `FullscreenPass::draw`, which is the one place every pass binds through. **It restores the caller's target, not the back buffer** — read back from the device rather than assumed — because a pass can run inside another pass's scope, which the bloom pyramid actually does, and restoring the frame there would be a subtler version of the bug being fixed. The destructor never throws: it runs on the unwind path of whatever failed first, and a second exception there replaces a diagnosable error with `std::terminate`. 5 tests including the throw path and nested scopes. |
| MOD-204 | `detail::RenderTargetPool` — reusable, size/format-keyed target cache with explicit `reset()` | ✅ | Done. `RenderTargetPool`, keyed by size/format/depth/slot. Tested for reuse, for every key that must produce a *different* target, for `reset()`, and for rejecting a non-positive size. |
| MOD-205 | `detail::PingPongTargets` — 2 targets of identical size/format with `swap()`, built on the pool | ✅ | Done, **without a separate class**: the alternation lives in `PostProcessChain`, which is its only consumer, and is asserted directly (`EachPassReadsWhatThePreviousOneWroteAndNeverItsOwnTarget`). A standalone `PingPongTargets` with one caller would have been indirection, not structure. |
| MOD-206 | `detail::BlitPass` — the identity/pass-through pass used by every "capability missing" fallback (D1) | ✅ | Done. `BlitPass` — the identity pass every capability fallback and the final resolve use. Verified by pixel comparison, not by inspection. |
| MOD-207 | Document the texture-coordinate origin convention once (GL bottom-left vs D3D top-left) and where the flip is applied | ✅ | Done. Documented on `FullscreenPass`: the convention is resolved once inside `SpriteBatch`, which is a reason for the MOD-19 deviation rather than a separate mechanism. |
| MOD-208 | `PostProcessChain` — ordered container of passes with ping-pong management and one final resolve | ✅ | Done. `PostProcessChain` — N passes, N-1 intermediates, only the last writing the caller's destination, intermediates in the source's own format so an HDR chain cannot be silently clamped mid-way. |
| MOD-209 | Pass-level enable flags driven by `RenderPipelineSettings` (no branch inside shaders) | ✅ | `PassEnableFlagTests.cpp`, 5 cases with the counting fake the row names. What they pin is the distinction that a screenshot cannot show: a disabled pass is **removed from the chain**, not run with its effect switched off inside the shader — the latter still costs a fullscreen draw, a bind and a sampler read every frame, so a quality preset that disabled three passes would save nothing. Everything off gives `getLastFramePassCount() == 0` and no scene target at all; each flag adds exactly one pass, counted one at a time so a pass added twice shows up as arithmetic rather than as a slower frame; a user pass runs across all of it, proving "skipped" means skipped; and the flags can be flipped every frame for eight frames without rebuilding anything, which is what a settings menu does. |
| MOD-210 | `ShaderEffectFactory` helper — compiles a named pass shader once and caches it per device | ✅ | `CNA::Graphics::ShaderEffectFactory` (`modules/graphics-ext/{include,src}/CNA/Graphics/ShaderEffectFactory.{hpp,cpp}`), with a compile counter — which is how the row's criterion is actually checked, since a shader compiled twice looks identical and only costs twice. **Keyed by name, not by source**, and that is a rule rather than an oversight: hashing two kilobytes of GLSL to discover it is the same GLSL is work spent to avoid work. The consequence — a name must mean one shader, and reusing it with different source returns the first — is pinned by `TheNameIsTheKeyAndTheSourceIsNotConsulted` so it is documented behaviour rather than a surprise. A failed compile is cached and returned rather than nulled, so a caller has one failure mode (`IsEffectValid()`) instead of two, and a shader that will not compile is not recompiled on every request. 6 tests. |

### 2.2 `ShaderEffect` gaps (each becomes real work only if `MOD-16` finds it missing)

| ID | Task | Status | Acceptance criterion |
|---|---|---|---|
| MOD-215 | `ShaderEffect::SetUniformMatrix` (4×4, column-major) if absent | ✅ | Not needed — present already (MOD-16). |
| MOD-216 | `ShaderEffect` multi-sampler binding (≥4 texture units with named samplers) | ✅ | Not needed — present already (MOD-16). |
| MOD-217 | `ShaderEffect` float/vec2/**vec3** array uniforms | ✅ | **Closed as "not needed" by MOD-16's survey, then reopened and actually done.** A `vec3[]` uniform cannot be filled through `SetUniformFloatArray`: GL rejects the type mismatch and silently leaves the uniform at zero. That turned SSAO's 64-sample kernel into 64 samples at the origin — an image with no occlusion in it, while every unit test about the kernel still passed. `SetUniformVec3Array` added through `IEffectRenderer`, `ShaderEffect` and EasyGL. |
| MOD-218 | `ShaderEffect::SetUniformInt` (sample counts, mode switches) | ✅ | Not needed — present already (MOD-16). |
| MOD-219 | `ShaderEffect` compile-error surfacing with the shader name and line context | ✅ **logs, does not throw** | `ShaderEffect::GetCompileErrorEXT()` surfaces the renderer's log (the `IEffectRenderer` already had it; nothing above could read it), and `CNA::Graphics::detail::ReportShaderCompileFailure` prints it once, naming the pass — wired into `BloomPass` (three shaders), `TonemapPass`, `FxaaPass`, `SsaoPass` (two) and `ShaderEffectFactory`, at **construction** rather than in `apply`, since the failure happens once and a per-frame check would either spam the log or need a flag to avoid it. **Deviation from the row's "throws":** measuring the renderers made throwing the wrong choice. `GraphicsCapability::CustomEffects` is true on three renderers that never compile GLSL source — Vulkan takes SPIR-V, SOFTWARE and HEADLESS accept and ignore — so throwing on a failed compile would turn a documented capability boundary into a crash on all three. That is not hypothetical; it is the exact shape of the bug `MOD-1699` fixed. `ItDoesNotThrowWhateverTheRenderer` pins the decision. 5 tests. |
| MOD-220 | `ShaderEffect` — allow a pass to declare its own sampler filtering/addressing requirements | ✅ — and the premise was already true | `FullscreenPass::draw`/`drawOverCurrentTarget` take an optional `SamplerState*`, defaulting to null, and `BloomPass` passes `LinearClamp` at all four of its draws. **The finding is that this changed no behaviour**, and that is worth recording rather than quietly shipping: `SpriteBatch::Begin` already documents a null sampler as meaning `LinearClamp`, so the pyramid was being filtered correctly all along — by a default that had nothing to do with bloom. The value of the row is therefore not a fix but an attachment: bloom's requirement is now stated where bloom is, so a change to `SpriteBatch`'s default can no longer turn the pyramid into a mosaic silently. The `const_cast` at the call site is forced by the XNA-shaped API (`SamplerState::LinearClamp` is a static const; `Begin` takes a non-const pointer) and is documented in place. |

### 2.3 Verification

| ID | Task | Status | Acceptance criterion |
|---|---|---|---|
| MOD-225 | Unit tests for `RenderTargetPool` (keying, reuse, reset, leak count) | ✅ | Done — four `RenderTargetPoolTest` cases. |
| MOD-226 | Unit tests for `PingPongTargets` and `PostProcessChain` ordering | ✅ | Done — insertion order and the full ping-pong contract, plus owned/borrowed passes and rejected contexts. |
| MOD-227 | Unit tests for `PostProcessPass` fallback behavior on a non-shader renderer | ✅ | `PostProcessFallbackTest` in `ScopedRenderTargetTests.cpp`, 3 cases, using a pass whose `isSupported` is unconditionally false — so the unsupported path is exercised on **every** renderer rather than only where shaders happen to be missing. It asserts the three things that matter: `apply()` does not throw, the destination receives the source unchanged (checked texel by texel, not merely non-empty), and the fallback leaves no render target bound. The third also pins `isSupported`'s default against the two-part `MOD-1699` question rather than against `CustomEffects` alone. Verified on EasyGL 8/8, Software 8/8, Headless 7 pass + 1 skip, Stub 1 pass + 7 skip (it binds no render targets at all). |
| MOD-228 | Golden image: `BlitPass` identity through an HDR intermediate | ✅ | Done. `AChainOfCopiesIsStillTheIdentity` compares real pixels after three real passes; `AnHdrChainKeepsItsIntermediatesInFloat` proves an above-1.0 value survives two chained passes. |
| MOD-229 | Example `cnaext_postprocess_chain_test` — 3 stacked trivial passes on a live window | ✅ | `modules/graphics-ext/examples/cnaext_postprocess_chain_test.cpp`, registered as ctest `CNAEXT_PostProcessChain`; **9/9 checks**. The three stacked passes are deliberately **not** shader-based: the subject is the chain, and a shader marker would make the program skip on exactly the renderers where a chain bug would be least noticed. Instead each pass records what it was handed, so the program can assert the property a chain exists to provide and that no image comparison would reveal — pass 1 reads the chain's source, pass 2 reads *what pass 1 wrote*, pass 3 reads what pass 2 wrote, the last writes the chain's destination, and **no pass ever reads its own destination**. The addresses are printed, so a reader can see the ping-pong rather than take it on trust. |
| MOD-230 | Perf: measure per-pass cost at 1280×720 on the reference renderer | ✅ | `cnaext_postprocess_chain_test --benchmark`, recorded in `docs/cnaext-perf.md`. At 1280×720: blit **6.22 ms** (the floor — one fullscreen draw), tonemap **6.90** (×1.11), FXAA **12.99** (×2.09), bloom **28.61** (×4.60). Reported against a copy rather than in isolation, because the copy is what any pass has to clear. The conclusion Phase 7's presets are built on: **bloom alone costs more than the other three together**, and structurally so — it is a pyramid, not a pass — which makes `bloomIterations` the first setting a quality preset should reach for, while tonemapping is close enough to free that switching it off buys nothing. The ordering is a property of how many texels each pass touches and survives hardware even though the absolute numbers do not. |
| MOD-231 | Verify the existing `DepthEffect`/`CRTEffect`/`AsciiPostProcessEffect` can be adapted to `PostProcessPass` | ✅ | Analysed, and the answer split the three: **`DepthEffect` and `CRTEffect` adapt cleanly and needed no change at all**, because they are `Effect` subclasses and a fullscreen pass *is* "draw the source through an effect". **`AsciiPostProcessEffect` cannot go through the same adapter**, and the reason is worth recording rather than working around: it is not an `Effect`. It reads the source back to the CPU, quantises it into a glyph grid and re-uploads one textured quad per cell — a multi-step CPU+GPU pass, not a shader program `SpriteBatch` can bind. That is also precisely what lets it run on renderers with no shaders, which is the property it exists for. So `MOD-232` landed **two** adapters, not one wrapper per effect. |
| MOD-232 | Adapter: expose `DepthEffect`/`CRTEffect`/`AsciiPostProcessEffect` as `PostProcessPass` implementations | ✅ | `EffectPass` (`modules/graphics-ext/{include,src}/CNA/Graphics/EffectPass.{hpp,cpp}`) runs any `Effect` as a pass, borrowed or owned, and is therefore also the shortest route to a *custom* pass — a game with a shader and no interest in subclassing hands it here. `AsciiPass` drives the one that is not an `Effect`, binding the destination through `ScopedRenderTarget` since the effect draws wherever the device points. **One deliberate deviation from the base class:** `EffectPass::isSupported` asks `CustomEffects` alone, **not** the two-part `MOD-1699` question, because it runs whatever effect it was handed and a compiled or stock effect is real work on a renderer that never compiles GLSL source. `SupportIsTheCustomEffectsQuestionNotTheShaderSourceOne` pins that so it is not "tidied" back. `AsciiPass::isSupported` probes the texture readback it needs, since no capability describes it. 17 tests; the three effects themselves are untouched. |
| MOD-233 | `docs/cnaext-engine-layer.md`: "writing your own pass" section with a 20-line example | ✅ | `docs/cnaext-engine-layer.md` §"Writing your own pass". Two routes rather than one: `EffectPass` for anything that is already an `Effect` (no class needed, and the route the three pre-existing CNAEXT effects take), and a subclass where the pass needs state, several draws or its own targets — with a complete ~25-line `VignettePass` for the second. It ends with the four rules the layer holds itself to and the symptom of breaking each: allocate in the constructor and take scratch targets from the pool; never throw from `apply` because the renderer cannot do something; ask the two-part support question, and know when to override it; and never leave a render target bound, because that failure looks like the frame freezing rather than like an error. |

---

## Phase 3 — Tonemapping (`MOD-300`–`MOD-327`)

→ CNAEXT.md §5.2, N21. The first real pass, and the one that makes HDR visible.

| ID | Task | Status | Acceptance criterion |
|---|---|---|---|
| MOD-300 | `TonemapPass` class skeleton (`PostProcessPass`, owns its `ShaderEffect`) | ✅ | Done. `TonemapPass` owns its `ShaderEffect`; a shader that fails to compile makes `isSupported()` false and `apply()` copy, rather than throwing out of a constructor. |
| MOD-301 | Tonemap GLSL: `None` (clamp only) | ✅ | Done — clamp only, with gamma still applied (`None` means "no curve", not "no display encode"). |
| MOD-302 | Tonemap GLSL: `Reinhard` (`c/(1+c)`) | ✅ | Done — matches `c/(1+c)` to 1e-6 at five sampled values. |
| MOD-303 | Tonemap GLSL: `Filmic` (Hejl–Burgess-Dawson) | ✅ | Done — Hejl/Burgess-Dawson, and the gamma step is skipped for it, asserted directly. |
| MOD-304 | Tonemap GLSL: `Aces` (Narkowicz fit) | ✅ | Done — Narkowicz's fit, documented as the fit rather than the full ACES transform. |
| MOD-305 | Tonemap GLSL: `Uncharted2` (→ MOD-21, N02) | ✅ | Done — Hable's curve with the standard constants, normalized against `W = 11.2`. |
| MOD-306 | Exposure multiplier applied before the curve | ✅ | Done. `exposure=2` on input `x` equals `exposure=1` on `2x` for every curve — the check that catches exposure applied in the wrong place, which looks almost right for Reinhard. |
| MOD-307 | Gamma applied after the curve (except `Filmic`, per `MOD-303`) | ✅ | Done, including the `Filmic` exception in both the shader and the CPU reference. |
| MOD-308 | Auto-exposure decision: **out of scope for v1**, documented | ⛔ | Not done, deliberately — auto-exposure needs a luminance reduction over the whole frame, which is a compute or mip-chain problem rather than a tonemapping one. Revisit at MOD-1552. |
| MOD-309 | `TonemapPass` reads `RenderPipelineSettings` (mode, exposure, gamma) each apply | ✅ | Done — mode, exposure and gamma are read from the context's settings on every apply, so changing them between frames changes the output with no reconstruction. |
| MOD-310 | Standalone use: tonemap any `Texture2D` to any `RenderTarget2D` or the backbuffer | ✅ | Done — the pass carries its own mode/exposure/gamma for use without a settings bag (D9). |
| MOD-311 | LDR input guard: tonemapping a `Color` source is legal and documented (values are simply ≤1) | ✅ | Done — an LDR `Color` source with mode `None` and gamma 1.0 round-trips within one 8-bit step, which is what the HDR-off pipeline depends on. |
| MOD-312 | Unit tests: all 5 modes × {exposure 0.5,1,4} × {gamma 1.0,2.2} numeric checks | ✅ | Done — 11 cases covering all five operators, exposure, gamma, monotonicity/boundedness, and that the four curves genuinely differ from one another. |
| MOD-313 | CPU reference implementations of the 5 curves (test-only helper) | ✅ | Done, **as public API rather than a test-only helper**: `TonemapPass::tonemapChannel()`. It is the only way to state "the shader agrees with the specification" as an assertion, and a game can use it for a UI preview without a GPU round trip. |
| MOD-314 | Golden image: HDR gradient tonemapped in each mode | ⛔ | No goldens, the same decision as `MOD-852`/`MOD-912`/`MOD-1009`/`MOD-1112`/`MOD-1242` and recorded once in `MOD-1703`. Five committed images of a tonemapped gradient would pin llvmpipe's exact rasterisation, not the operators. What a reader would inspect them *for* is asserted directly by `cnaext_tonemap_test` and across the whole ramp rather than at sampled points: every operator is monotonic (a non-monotonic curve gives banding and inverted highlights that one sample cannot reveal), `None` clips where the others compress, and the five are genuinely different curves rather than one curve renamed. |
| MOD-315 | Example `cnaext_tonemap_test` — live window, key-switchable mode/exposure | ✅ | `modules/graphics-ext/examples/cnaext_tonemap_test.cpp`, registered as ctest `CNAEXT_Tonemap`; **7/7 checks**. Not key-switchable: a program that needs a human at a keyboard is not a check, and the interesting thing about five operators is how they *differ*, which a program can render all five of and compare. It prints the first/middle/last of each curve so a reader sees the shape. |
| MOD-316 | Document the tonemapping contract (input linear HDR, output display-encoded) | ✅ | `docs/cnaext-engine-layer.md` §"Tonemapping: what goes in, what comes out, and what CNA does not do". Linear scene-referred in, display-encoded out, and that boundary is why the pass order around it is fixed — scene-value passes before, displayed-pixel passes after. It states the absence explicitly, because a tonemapper is exactly where colour management would live in a renderer that had it: CNA does **no** colour-space management beyond gamma — no sRGB-versus-Display-P3, no white-point adaptation, no ICC, no HDR10 or scRGB output. Includes the measured behaviour of all five operators at a scene value of 8.0. |
| MOD-317 | `TonemappingMode` ordinal-stability test (existing values must not shift) | ✅ | Done — covered by `RenderPipelineSettingsTest.TonemappingModeOrdinalsAreStable` (MOD-21). |
| MOD-318 | Verify tonemap output against the 2D-only fallback (blit) so a `SpriteBatch` game sees no change | ✅ — and it caught a regression | The identity check runs **first and unconditionally**, before the capability gate, because it is the promise made to games that link this layer and enable nothing: with HDR off and mode `None`, the frame is bit-identical to the same scene drawn with no pipeline at all (0 differing texels of 4096) and no scene target is allocated. It is also guarded against the way such a check usually goes wrong — a companion assertion that the gradient *is* a gradient, so the comparison is of an image and not of two black frames. **And it found a real regression, introduced two rows earlier by `MOD-203`:** `ScopedRenderTarget` restores whatever a pass found bound, so the chain, entered with the scene target still bound from `begin()`, faithfully put it back after the last pass wrote the back buffer — and the next `Present` refused with "Cannot present while render targets are bound". `RenderPipeline::end()` now unbinds the scene target before running the chain, which it should have done regardless: every pass *samples* that target, and sampling a bound render target is undefined in GL. |
| MOD-319 | Perf: tonemap cost at 720p/1080p | ✅ | `cnaext_tonemap_test --benchmark`, in `docs/cnaext-perf.md`: **7.78 ms** at 1280×720 and **16.82 ms** at 1920×1080. 1080p is 2.25× the pixels and costs 2.16× the time — it scales with pixel count and nothing else, which is exactly what one dependent texture read and a curve per texel should do. There is no resolution at which tonemapping becomes the problem. |
| MOD-320 | Wire `RenderQuality` presets to tonemap precision decisions (none today — document why) | ✅ ⛔ by decision | Documented in `docs/cnaext-engine-layer.md` §Tonemapping: **`RenderQuality` does not affect tonemapping**, with three reasons rather than an empty cell. There is nothing to turn down — the operator is one curve per texel, with no sample count and no resolution to lower, since the pass must touch every pixel of the frame it is display-encoding. Its measured cost is close to a plain copy, so there would be little to win. And the operator is an **artistic** choice: silently changing the curve because a player selected "Low" would change how the game looks, which is not what a quality preset is for. |

---

## Phase 4 — Bloom (`MOD-400`–`MOD-431`)

→ CNAEXT.md §5.2, N22. Promotes the existing EasyGL example GLSL to library code.

| ID | Task | Status | Acceptance criterion |
|---|---|---|---|
| MOD-400 | Extract the bloom GLSL from `easygl_bloom_*` examples into a shared, documented source location | ✅ | Done — the GLSL is written fresh as library code in `BloomPass.cpp`; the examples stay as they are. |
| MOD-401 | `BloomPass` skeleton + settings (threshold, intensity, radius, iterations) | ✅ | Done. `BloomPass` reads threshold, intensity and iteration count from settings, and carries its own values for standalone use. |
| MOD-402 | Bright-pass extract shader (threshold with soft knee) | ✅ | Done — soft knee, because a hard cut-off makes bloom pop in and out as a highlight crosses the threshold, which is far more visible in motion than the energy missing just below it. Tested through `extractChannel`. |
| MOD-403 | Downsample chain (½ resolution per level, configurable level count 1–6) | ✅ | Done — half-resolution extract, then halving per iteration, clamped to 1..8 and stopped when a step would fall below 2 pixels. |
| MOD-404 | Separable Gaussian blur (horizontal + vertical) with a documented tap count per quality | ✅ | Done — separable 9-tap Gaussian, horizontal then vertical (18 samples where a 9×9 kernel costs 81). |
| MOD-405 | Upsample + additive combine chain | ✅ | `BloomPass::apply` now keeps every pyramid level and walks back up, adding each into the one above before the final composite, through a new `kUpsampleSource` shader. What it replaced — a single composite of the *smallest* level — is the simpler thing and gives a wide but **flat** glow, because the tighter core the larger levels still carry is thrown away on the way down. "Visually verified" was not enough to check that, so it is measured instead: `BloomPyramidTest.MoreLevelsReachFurtherFromTheSource` asserts that more levels put light *further* from the source, which a single-composite pyramid cannot do since its widest level dominates every configuration. Two more properties come with it — every level adds light rather than removing it, and the halo is symmetric to within 15%, which catches a half-texel offset in the upsample that would otherwise read as a lighting choice. |
| MOD-406 | Final composite into the destination (`scene + bloom*intensity`) | ✅ | Done. `intensity = 0` reproduces the scene exactly — the strongest available statement that the composite keeps the scene intact. |
| MOD-407 | Linear-filter fallback when float linear filtering is unavailable (`MOD-123`) | ✅ | The upsample shader takes a `uManualFilter` branch: four taps averaged by hand where `GraphicsCapability::HalfFloatTextureLinearFiltering` is false. Asked **once at construction**, since it is a property of the renderer and not of the frame. **Documented quality difference:** it is a box filter rather than the hardware's bilinear one, so slightly blockier — and deliberately not the "nearest sample" the row proposed, which makes the upsample visibly stair-step. No setting exposes it, because there is no reason to prefer the worse path where the better one exists. |
| MOD-408 | LDR bloom path (works on `Color` targets when HDR is off) | ✅ | Done — bloom is an HDR effect but not an HDR-only one; on an 8-bit target a threshold below 1.0 still produces a usable glow. |
| MOD-409 | `RenderQuality` → level count / tap count mapping | ✅ | `BloomPass::iterationsForQuality` — Low 2, Medium 3, High 5, Ultra 7 — plus `RenderPipelineSettings::applyRenderQualityPresetEXT()`. **The mapping is a function, not a side effect:** `setRenderQuality` stores a preference and rewrites nothing, so a game that tuned `bloomIterations` by hand keeps its value, and a settings menu that wants the preset applied says so in one call. 5 tests, including that separation and that the presets stay inside the range `apply()` accepts — a preset silently clamped into a different preset would be worse than no preset. The example checks the presets are *observable*: each higher one spreads at least as much light, and Ultra genuinely beats Low. |
| MOD-410 | Target pool reuse — all bloom mip targets come from `RenderTargetPool` | ✅ | Done — all intermediates come from a `RenderTargetPool` and are reused across frames. |
| MOD-411 | Resize handling — recreate the chain on viewport change without leaking | ✅ | Done — `resetTargets()`, called by `RenderPipeline::resize`. |
| MOD-412 | Unit tests: threshold/knee math, kernel weights, level-count clamping | ✅ | Done — threshold/knee behaviour, a zero threshold (which must not divide by zero), and iteration clamping. |
| MOD-413 | Golden image: a bright quad on a dark field, 3 intensity values | ⛔ | No goldens, the decision recorded in `MOD-1703`. Three committed images of a bright quad would pin llvmpipe's rasterisation, and the tolerance the row asks for is exactly the admission that they would not survive a driver change. What a reader would inspect them for is asserted directly by `cnaext_bloom_test` at three intensities and by `BloomPyramidTest` — the glow exists, grows with intensity, vanishes above the threshold, reaches further with more levels, and is symmetric. |
| MOD-414 | Golden image: bloom disabled == input | ✅ | Done — `ZeroIntensityReproducesTheSceneExactly`. |
| MOD-415 | Example `cnaext_bloom_test` — live window with adjustable threshold/intensity | ✅ | `cnaext_bloom_test` extended from 4 checks to **8**, still registered as ctest `CNAEXT_BloomInTenLines`. Adjustable rather than key-switchable, for the reason `MOD-315` records: a program needing a human at a keyboard is not a check. It now sweeps the threshold — above the scene's own brightness the glow must vanish **entirely**, which is the only check here that would fail if the extract stage were a no-op — and all four quality presets, printing each one's level count and resulting glow. |
| MOD-416 | Perf: bloom cost per quality preset at 720p/1080p | ✅ — and it contradicted the preset table | `cnaext_bloom_test --benchmark`, recorded in `docs/cnaext-perf.md`. At 1280×720: Low **11.92 ms**, Medium **12.70**, High **14.82**, Ultra **15.50**. **The level count is a weak dial for cost** — Low to Ultra is 30%, not the threefold difference "quality" suggests — because the first level is half-resolution and dominates while every level after it is a quarter of the one before. The preset table in `BloomPass.hpp` was drafted claiming Low cost "roughly a third of Ultra" and has been **corrected to the measurement**, with the conclusion stated where a reader will meet it: the preset buys halo *width*, and a frame that cannot afford bloom cannot afford it at Low either — the answer there is `setBloomEnabled(false)`, which costs nothing at all. |
| MOD-417 | Document bloom's energy behavior (not physically normalized; intensity is artistic) | ✅ | `docs/cnaext-engine-layer.md` §"Bloom: what the numbers mean, and what they do not". The stated fact is that bloom here is **not** physically normalised: it adds the blurred highlights on top of an untouched scene rather than redistributing energy, so `intensity` adds light to the frame. Written down because "intensity 1.0" looks like it ought to mean something physical and does not. With it, the consequence for reproducibility: the value is portable across renderers for the same scene, because the maths is identical everywhere, but is not comparable with another engine's slider and interacts with exposure — so it should be tuned against a tonemapped frame. |
| MOD-418 | Cross-check the promoted GLSL against the original example output | ✅ | Done differently from the plan: the pass is verified by measurement rather than by comparison with the example — a pixel that was exactly black before it is non-black after, and a higher intensity produces more of it. That states what bloom must do; an image comparison would only state that two implementations agree. |

---

## Phase 5 — Depth/normal prepass + SSAO (`MOD-500`–`MOD-539`)

→ CNAEXT.md §5.2, N23. SSAO needs scene depth and normals, which nothing in CNA currently exposes to
a post-process. Half of this phase is that plumbing; it is also what `MOD-1400`'s culling and future
depth-based effects will reuse.

### 5.1 Depth/normal availability

| ID | Task | Status | Acceptance criterion |
|---|---|---|---|
| MOD-500 | Decide the strategy: **explicit depth+normal prepass into an MRT** vs sampling the depth attachment | ✅ | **Explicit prepass**, written up in `docs/cnaext-engine-layer.md` §"The depth/normal prepass". Sampling the depth attachment would be free and is not portable: several renderers never expose it as a texture, the ones that do disagree about its precision and about whether it is readable while still bound, and the value in it is non-linear differently per API. Drawing the geometry a second time costs a pass and is identical everywhere. The row also offered "into an MRT" as part of the choice; that turned out to be a *second*, independent decision — the prepass uses MRT where the renderer has it and two passes where it does not, which is why `getPassCount()` exists rather than a capability gate. |
| MOD-501 | `DepthNormalPrepass` — renders the scene into `RG16F` linear depth + `Color` view-space normals | ✅ | `CNA::Graphics::DepthNormalPrepass` (`modules/graphics-ext/{include,src}/CNA/Graphics/DepthNormalPrepass.{hpp,cpp}`), shaped like `ShadowMap` as the row asks: `begin`/`end` around the app's own scene draw. **The one shape difference is the loop:** `begin` takes a pass index and `getPassCount()` is 1 with MRT and 2 without, so an app that writes the loop is correct on both. Depth is `HalfSingle` (or packed `Color`), normals are `Color`. 11 tests including every misuse — end without begin, two passes at once, an out-of-range index, a resize mid-pass — and the begin path opens the pass only after the bind succeeds, so a renderer that refuses a target does not leave the object permanently "already open". |
| MOD-502 | `DepthNormalEffect` (CNAEXT, XNA namespace) — the effect the prepass swaps in | ✅ **as a `ShaderEffect`, not a new XNA class** | **Deviation, following the precedent `MOD-1101` set for the sky.** A `DepthNormalEffect` in the XNA namespace would need a matching program added to every renderer before the prepass could run at all; a `ShaderEffect` owned by the prepass runs wherever custom effects run and is what the row actually needs. Documented encoding: linear view depth normalised by the far plane, and `n * 0.5 + 0.5` view-space normals. One fragment shader serves all four combinations (packed/float × MRT/single-target) by branching on two uniforms — a driver folds uniform-controlled branches, and four near-identical shaders would be four places for the encoding to drift. |
| MOD-503 | Skinned variant of the prepass effect (bone transforms) so skinned meshes occlude correctly | ✅ | A second `ShaderEffect` with the bone palette, sharing the fragment shader and the `uBones[72]`/`uWeightsPerVertex` convention the shadow casters already use — 72 is `SkinnedEffect::MaxBones`, so a mesh that fits one fits the other. `TheSkinnedEffectIsASecondProgramNotTheSameOne` asserts both compiled and that they are distinct programs. The failure it prevents is worth naming: a skinned mesh drawn with the rigid effect lands in the depth buffer in its **bind pose**, so it occludes the wrong part of the screen — which looks like the AO being wrong rather than the prepass being wrong. |
| MOD-504 | Linear-depth reconstruction helper GLSL (`viewZ` from the stored value, and world position from UV+depth) | ✅ | `DepthNormalPrepass::getDepthDecodeGlsl(packed)` returns `cnaDecodeLinearDepth(vec4)` and `cnaViewPositionFromDepth(vec2, float, mat4)` as one string a consumer concatenates. One shared function rather than a copy per effect, because the encoding and its inverse must agree and two copies that agree today are one edit from an SSAO that darkens the wrong pixels. The reconstruction scales the unprojected ray by `depth / -ray.z` rather than by its length, since the stored value is distance along view −Z, not distance from the eye. Unit-verified through the packing round trip below; the GLSL's own presence under both encodings is asserted. |
| MOD-505 | Camera parameters (`near`, `far`, `projection`, `inverseProjection`) delivered to passes via `PostProcessContext` | ✅ | Done for what SSAO actually needs: depth and normals travel through `PostProcessContext`, along with the projection fields. The camera block is populated by the caller rather than derived, because the pipeline never sees the camera. |
| MOD-506 | Renderer capability check for MRT (`MultipleRenderTargets`) with a documented 2-pass fallback | ✅ | Done differently: the pass takes depth and normals as two textures and does not care whether they were produced by one MRT pass or two, so the MRT-versus-two-pass choice belongs to whatever draws them rather than to SSAO. |
| MOD-507 | Prepass target formats fall back to `Color`-packed depth when float RTs are missing | ✅ — and it found a real inversion | Packed into a `Color` target where `HalfSingle` is unavailable, with the packing written once as GLSL and once as C++ (`packDepth`/`unpackDepth`) and a 513-point sweep asserting they agree — the failure a packed format always has is halves that agree on the shifts but not on the mask, so mid-range values come back wrong while 0 and 1 look fine. **The sweep caught something worse:** `fract(1.0)` is zero, so an unclamped far-plane depth packed to all-zeroes and read back as the *nearest* possible surface. Depth is normalised by the far plane, which makes 1.0 the most common value in the buffer — the whole background would have rendered in front of the scene. Both encoders now stop one texel short of 1.0, and the test pins that explicitly rather than hiding it in the sweep. Quality difference recorded: the packed path is *more* precise than a half-float (24 bits against 11 of mantissa), banded differently, and costs arithmetic at both ends. |

### 5.2 SSAO

| ID | Task | Status | Acceptance criterion |
|---|---|---|---|
| MOD-515 | `SsaoPass` skeleton + settings (radius, intensity, bias, sample count, power) | ✅ | Done. `SsaoPass` reads radius, intensity and sample count from settings and carries its own for standalone use. |
| MOD-516 | Hemisphere sample-kernel generation (deterministic, seeded) | ✅ | Done — a deterministic low-discrepancy hemisphere set (Van der Corput), biased toward the origin so nearby geometry dominates. Asserted: every sample is in the +Z hemisphere, none longer than unit, and the early quarter is closer to the origin than the late quarter. |
| MOD-517 | Rotation-noise texture (4×4 tiled) | ✅ | Done — a fixed 4×4 rotation texture. Deterministic on purpose: the pass must produce the same image twice. |
| MOD-518 | SSAO GLSL: occlusion estimate with range check | ✅ | Done, with a range check so a distant silhouette cannot darken the surface in front of it. Verified on synthetic inputs: a flat wall stays unoccluded, a depth step darkens the surface beside it. |
| MOD-519 | Bilateral/box blur pass for the AO buffer | ✅ | Done — a 5×5 blur folded into the composite pass rather than a separate one, which would need a third intermediate for no gain at this kernel size. |
| MOD-520 | AO application mode: multiply into the scene's ambient term only, not into direct light | ✅ | Done as a screen-space multiply, documented as the approximation it is: it darkens direct light along with ambient. Doing it correctly means feeding AO into each lit effect's ambient term — a change to every lit shader, not a post-process. |
| MOD-521 | Optional: feed AO into `PbrEffect`'s occlusion slot instead of a screen-space multiply | ⛔ **investigated and refused** | The slot exists and would be the physically right place — the PBR shader multiplies it into the *ambient* term only, which is what `MOD-520`'s screen-space multiply approximates. It cannot take this buffer: `uOcclusionMap` is sampled at the mesh's **UV coordinates**, because it is a per-material texture in texture space, while screen-space AO is indexed by screen position. Binding one to the other samples the AO buffer with the model's UVs — wrong in a way no tuning recovers. Doing it properly needs three things this layer does not have: a `gl_FragCoord`-based sampler added to the PBR shader **in every PBR-capable renderer**, the AO buffer ready *before* the forward pass rather than after it (a second prepass, or a deferred pipeline), and a way to say "this occlusion is screen-space" that the glTF material model has no field for. Refused rather than deferred: the screen-space multiply is a deliberate approximation with a documented cost, not a placeholder for this. |
| MOD-522 | `RenderQuality` → sample count mapping (8/16/32/64) | ✅ | `SsaoPass::sampleCountForQuality` — Low 8, Medium 16, High 32, Ultra 64 — applied by the same `applyRenderQualityPresetEXT()` bloom uses, so one call sets both subsystems and neither is touched until it is made. Documented and tested, including that no preset is a value the pass would clamp (a clamped preset is silently a different preset). **Unlike bloom's level count this is a real performance dial**, which `MOD-528` measured: 8 to 64 samples is 3.4× the time, because the shader loops over the kernel per texel. |
| MOD-523 | Half-resolution AO option with upsample | ✅ | `SsaoPass::setHalfResolution`, **off by default**. The occlusion buffer is computed at half size and the compose pass's bilinear read is the upsample. Two details the row did not name and that the implementation needs: the rotation-noise tiling scales against the *occlusion buffer* rather than the frame, or the 4×4 pattern repeats twice as often and becomes visible cross-hatching; and the folded blur steps by the occlusion buffer's texels, so it covers the same *screen* distance either way instead of halving with the buffer. Quality difference documented — AO is low-frequency, so this costs far less than the pixel count suggests, but thin contact shadows lose definition, which is exactly where AO earns its keep. Both paths are asserted to produce occlusion, since a half-resolution path that silently produced nothing would look like AO merely being weak. |
| MOD-524 | Unit tests: kernel generation, noise texture, settings round-trip, capability fallback | ✅ | Done — 8 cases: kernel distribution and determinism, flat-surface and discontinuity behaviour, intensity, the missing-input fallback, and settings round-trip. |
| MOD-525 | Golden image: a sphere on a plane, AO visible in the contact region | ⛔ | No golden, the decision recorded in `MOD-1703`. The row itself names the reason it would not work: AO is noise-sensitive, and the tolerance a committed image would need is the admission that it pins llvmpipe's rasterisation rather than the occlusion. `cnaext_ssao_test` asserts the properties instead — a depth step produces occlusion, a frame without one produces measurably less, intensity scales it, zero intensity is the unoccluded frame, and every quality preset occludes. |
| MOD-526 | Golden image: SSAO disabled == input | ✅ | Asserted in `cnaext_ssao_test`, **first and unconditionally**, before the capability gate: 0 differing texels of 4096 against the same scene drawn with no pipeline at all. Guarded against the way an identity check usually goes wrong, with a companion assertion that the scene is lit — otherwise two black frames would pass it. |
| MOD-527 | Example `cnaext_ssao_test` — live window, radius/intensity adjustable | ✅ | `modules/graphics-ext/examples/cnaext_ssao_test.cpp`, registered as ctest `CNAEXT_Ssao`; **9/9 checks**. The first program that drives the whole chain the way a game does — prepass, then the two images into the pipeline. Radius and intensity are swept rather than made interactive, for the reason `MOD-315` records. The check worth naming is the *pair*: SSAO must darken a frame whose depth has a step **and** leave a frame without one alone. A pass that darkened everything uniformly would satisfy either one alone. |
| MOD-528 | Perf: prepass + SSAO + blur cost per quality at 720p/1080p | ✅ | `cnaext_ssao_test --benchmark`, recorded in `docs/cnaext-perf.md`. At 1280×720: Low **28.71 ms**, Medium **45.62**, High **58.85**, Ultra **97.39**; at 1080p, 63.71 to 222.07. **SSAO is by a wide margin the most expensive pass in this layer** — Medium costs more than bloom at Ultra, and about six times the tonemapper — because every sample is a dependent texture read across the whole frame. The prepass measures ~0.01 ms and that figure is reported as a **floor** rather than a cost: the benchmark draws no geometry into it, so what is timed is the bind and the clear. It is recorded anyway because it settles which half to look at — if a prepass is expensive it is the geometry, and the answer is fewer draws. |
| MOD-529 | Document the whole depth/normal contract in `docs/cnaext-engine-layer.md` | ✅ | `docs/cnaext-engine-layer.md` gains two sections: §"The depth/normal prepass, and what a game has to do" (the loop an app writes, both encodings with their clear values and *why* those values, the two shared GLSL decoders, and the two limits — packed depth reaching one texel short of 1.0, and non-uniform scale skewing the normal) and §"SSAO: what it approximates, and where AO is applied". **What happens if the game does not drive the prepass** is stated plainly, because it is the failure most likely to be misread: `SsaoPass` reports `isSupported() == false` and the chain copies through, so the frame renders *without* AO. It does not throw and does not go black — which means a missing prepass looks like "SSAO is not doing much" rather than like an error. |

---

## Phase 6 — FXAA and post-AA (`MOD-600`–`MOD-619`)

→ CNAEXT.md §5.2, N24. Matters most on renderers that cannot MSAA a float render target.

| ID | Task | Status | Acceptance criterion |
|---|---|---|---|
| MOD-600 | `FxaaPass` skeleton + settings (quality preset, edge thresholds) | ✅ | Done. `FxaaPass` with a configurable edge threshold, defaulting to the usual 0.125. |
| MOD-601 | FXAA 3.11-style GLSL (luma-based edge detect + directional blend), documented as a reimplementation | ✅ | Done — written from the description of the technique rather than ported: local luminance range, an early out below the threshold, then a blend along the edge direction, with the wider blend rejected when it leaves the local luminance range (which is the filter reaching past the edge). |
| MOD-602 | Luma source decision (computed from RGB vs stored in alpha) and documentation | ✅ | Done — luminance is computed from RGB. The alpha-luma optimization needs every earlier pass to maintain it, which nothing here does; noted rather than half-implemented. |
| MOD-603 | Apply FXAA **after** tonemapping (documented ordering rule) | ✅ | Done — the pipeline runs FXAA after tonemapping. On scene-referred values a highlight ten times brighter than white reads as an enormous edge and is blurred into its surroundings. |
| MOD-604 | Quality presets mapped from `RenderQuality` | ✅ | `FxaaPass::edgeThresholdForQuality` — Low 0.250, Medium 0.125, High 0.0625, Ultra 0.0312 (the FXAA reference's own quality value) — carried through `RenderPipelineSettings::getFXAAEdgeThresholdEXT()` so `applyRenderQualityPresetEXT()` sets all three subsystems in one call. **A gap closed on the way:** `FxaaPass` was the only pass not reading its parameter from the settings bag when one was supplied, so a pipeline that applied a quality preset was silently overruled by the pass's own default. 7 tests, including that the direction is not inverted — four distinct thresholds in the wrong order would make "Ultra" the preset that filters least, which a test of the numbers alone would not catch. |
| MOD-605 | Unit tests: settings, support, disabled == input | ✅ | Done — threshold round trip, the disabled case, and both halves of the contract below. |
| MOD-606 | Golden image: aliased triangle before/after | ✅ | Done by measurement rather than by golden image: a strictly black-and-white staircase gains pixels strictly between the two (the definition of a smoothed edge), while a flat field comes back unchanged — an edge filter that also softens flat areas is a blur. |
| MOD-607 | Example `cnaext_fxaa_test` toggling FXAA live | ✅ — and its first version measured nothing | `modules/graphics-ext/examples/cnaext_fxaa_test.cpp`, ctest `CNAEXT_Fxaa`, **8/8 checks**. It measures the artefact rather than the picture: total contrast between vertically adjacent texels along a drawn staircase *is* the aliasing. **The preset check had to be rewritten:** its first version used the white staircase, whose steps are maximum contrast, so all four presets filtered it identically and the check passed at 15937 four times over while measuring nothing. It now uses a faint (grey 25, luma ≈ 0.098) staircase that straddles the thresholds, and the presets separate exactly where they should — Ultra and High filter it, Medium and Low leave it alone. The pair of checks that matters is C and D together: FXAA must reduce contrast on an edge **and** leave a flat field bit-identical, which is what stops it from being a full-screen blur. |
| MOD-608 | Perf: FXAA cost at 720p/1080p | ✅ — and it contradicted the preset's premise | Measured on two deliberately opposite images, because FXAA's cost is decided by how often its early exit is taken and a single number would describe one picture rather than the pass. At 1280×720 the four presets are 3.61/3.64/3.24/4.02 ms on a flat field and 3.49/3.82/3.34/3.55 ms on a one-pixel checkerboard; at 1080p, 8.20 and 8.13. **Neither axis separates** — that spread is the noise of a 20-frame sample, not a trend. So the FXAA preset buys **look and nothing else**, which makes it the only preset in this layer that is not also a performance dial. The reason is in the shader, not the driver: five of its eleven samples happen *before* the threshold test, because the test needs the neighbourhood it is testing, so the exit saves the later six and that is not enough to show up on a fill-bound pass. The header doc claimed the opposite and is corrected. |
| MOD-609 | Document when to prefer MSAA over FXAA per renderer | ✅ | `docs/cnaext-engine-layer.md` §"FXAA, and when to prefer MSAA instead" — a five-row comparison and the guidance that goes with it. The point that decides it more often than cost: **MSAA is the only one of the two that antialiases correctly in HDR**, because it resolves before tonemapping, where FXAA necessarily runs after it on displayed pixels. Averaging four samples of a highlight in scene-referred space is not the same as blurring the tonemapped result, and on a bright edge the difference is visible. Also recorded: using both is legitimate rather than redundant, and the practical per-renderer position — EasyGL has both, Vulkan has MSAA but not FXAA (its `ShaderEffect` takes SPIR-V), and the 2D-only identities have neither. |
| MOD-610 | Decide on SMAA/TAA: ⛔ out of scope for this plan, with reasons | ⛔ | Confirmed out of scope for this plan. TAA needs motion vectors and a history buffer — a different pipeline shape, not a pass — and SMAA needs a precomputed lookup texture this layer has no asset path for. |

---

## Phase 7 — `RenderPipeline` orchestrator (`MOD-700`–`MOD-747`)

→ CNAEXT.md §5.1, N20/N25. The class that finally gives `RenderPipelineSettings` a consumer.

### 7.1 Core

| ID | Task | Status | Acceptance criterion |
|---|---|---|---|
| MOD-700 | `RenderPipeline` skeleton: ctor(device), dtor, non-copyable, `getSettings()` | ✅ | Done. Constructs on any renderer, non-copyable, and allocates nothing until `resize()` — the size is not known and the format depends on settings the caller has not made yet. |
| MOD-701 | `resize(width,height)` — allocate/reallocate HDR scene target + aux targets | ✅ | Done. Idempotent for a size it already holds; drops the old scene target and the chain's intermediates, so a resized game does not keep paying for every size it has been. 20 alternating resizes stay bounded. |
| MOD-702 | HDR scene target creation (`HalfVector4` when supported, `Color` otherwise, logged once) | ✅ | Done. `HdrBlendable` when the renderer has it, `Vector4` next, `Color` otherwise — and `getSceneTargetFormat()` reports what was really created rather than what was asked for. |
| MOD-703 | `begin(clearColor)` — bind the scene target and clear (colour + depth) | ✅ | Done. Binds the scene target and clears it; the frame's draws land there, verified through `getSceneTarget()`. |
| MOD-704 | `end()` — run the fixed chain and resolve to the backbuffer | ✅ | Done. `end()` builds the chain in fixed order and resolves to the back buffer. Tonemap first, then user passes — asserted by pass counts and a counting pass. |
| MOD-705 | Fixed chain order documented and justified | ✅ | Done — the reason is recorded where the order is built: tonemapping is the boundary between scene-referred and display-referred colour, so scene-value passes precede it and pixel passes follow. |
| MOD-706 | `addUserPass(PostProcessPass*)` / `removeUserPass` — app-supplied passes at a documented slot | ✅ | Done for the borrowed form (`addUserPass`/`clearUserPasses`), which is what a game keeping its own configurable pass needs. An owning overload is not added until something needs it. |
| MOD-707 | HDR-off path: no HDR target, no tonemap, chain reduced to enabled passes | ✅ | Done — with HDR off and no tonemapping the chain is empty and the scene target is skipped entirely. |
| MOD-708 | Zero-pass short circuit: when nothing is enabled and HDR is off, render straight to the backbuffer | ✅ | Done, and this is the row that makes the layer safe to adopt: an inert pipeline allocates no target, runs no pass, and renders straight to the back buffer. Asserted through the memory estimate, the pass count and `isUsingSceneTarget()`. |
| MOD-709 | `setShadowCaster(DirectionalLightEXT*)` (Phase 8 consumer) | ✅ | `RenderPipeline::setShadowScene(shadowMap, light, sceneBounds, drawCasters)` rather than the row's `setShadowCaster(DirectionalLightEXT*)`, and the difference is the row's own acceptance note made explicit: the shadow pass runs *inside* `begin()`, before it returns, so the pipeline needs the caster **draw** as well as the light — a light pointer alone leaves nothing to render into the map. Passing a null shadow map clears it. Documented in `docs/cnaext-engine-layer.md` §Shadows, and `RenderPipelineTest.EachMissingIngredientLeavesTheShadowPassUnrun` covers each missing piece separately. |
| MOD-710 | `setSkybox(Skybox*)` (Phase 11 consumer) | 🟨 | Superseded in part: the pipeline's pass ordering is implemented and bloom is wired in ahead of tonemapping (bloom reasons about scene-referred values that tonemapping compresses away). The skybox consumer itself waits for Phase 11. |
| MOD-711 | `setDepthNormalPrepass(...)` wiring for SSAO (Phase 5 consumer) | ✅ | Done. `RenderPipeline::setDepthNormalInputs()` — the pipeline cannot render these itself (that means drawing the game's geometry again with a different effect, which only the game can do), so it takes them and SSAO renders an unoccluded frame when they are absent. |
| MOD-712 | `getSceneTarget()` accessor so apps can sample the HDR scene from custom passes | ✅ | Done — `getSceneTarget()` returns null outside `begin`/`end`, and null when the pipeline short-circuited. |
| MOD-713 | Exception safety: an exception inside a pass restores the backbuffer binding and rethrows | ✅ — already true, now pinned | No code change was needed, and finding that out is the point: `ScopedRenderTarget` (`MOD-203`) already restores the binding on the unwind path, and `end()` clears `frameOpen_` before running the chain. `PipelineExceptionSafetyTest` proves both with a throwing fake pass — after the exception nothing is bound, and **the pipeline still works**: the next `begin()` is accepted and the next frame renders. The second half is the one that would have been missed by a test that only checked the binding: an `end()` that threw with `frameOpen_` still set would leave the object permanently unusable, which is the trap `ShadowMap` and `CubeShadowMap` both had. |
| MOD-714 | `begin()`/`end()` misuse guards (double begin, end without begin) | ✅ | Done — double `begin()`, `end()` without `begin()`, and `begin()` before any `resize()` all throw `std::logic_error`. |
| MOD-715 | `GraphicsDevice` device-reset/context-loss handling — reallocate targets on reset | ✅ | `RenderPipeline` subscribes to `GraphicsDevice::DeviceReset` and drops its scene target and the chain's pool, so the next frame reallocates. Exposed as `releaseDeviceResourcesEXT()` too, for a game that recreates its device by another route. **It refuses mid-frame**, because releasing the scene target while it is bound is the exact situation this exists to avoid. **And the destructor unsubscribes** — the handler captures `this`, and a device outliving a pipeline is the *normal* case since `Game` owns the device, so without it a later reset calls into freed memory: a use-after-free ASan would catch and an ordinary run would not. 4 tests, including that one. Verified by raising the event rather than by `DebugSimulateContextLoss()`, which is a renderer-internal seam the engine layer has no route to. |
| MOD-716 | `getGpuMemoryEstimateBytes()` (→ MOD-139) | ✅ | Done — `getGpuMemoryEstimateBytes()` sums the scene target and the chain's pool. |
| MOD-717 | `getStatistics()` — passes run, draw calls, target switches for the last frame | ✅ | `RenderPipeline::FrameStatistics` + `getStatistics()`: passes run, target switches, whether a scene target was used, whether the sky drew, and the memory estimate. A POD returned by value, so a caller can keep last frame's numbers to compare with this frame's — asserted, since accessors would not allow it. **Deliberately not a profiler:** there are no timings. Timing a pass meaningfully needs a GPU query the renderer contracts do not have, and a CPU stopwatch around a call that only *queues* work reports a number that looks precise and means nothing. Target switches are derived (2 + passes) rather than counted by a device hook, so no renderer pays for a diagnostic it is not using. |

### 7.2 Settings integration

| ID | Task | Status | Acceptance criterion |
|---|---|---|---|
| MOD-725 | Settings changes take effect on the next frame without reconstruction | ✅ | Done — toggling settings between frames changes the next frame with no reconstruction. |
| MOD-726 | Changing `hdrEnabled` reallocates the scene target lazily | ✅ | Done — the scene target is reallocated only when the chosen format actually changes. |
| MOD-727 | `RenderQuality` preset application across all passes in one place | ✅ | `RenderPipelineSettings::applyRenderQualityPresetEXT()` — one call, three subsystems: `BloomPass::iterationsForQuality`, `SsaoPass::sampleCountForQuality`, `FxaaPass::edgeThresholdForQuality`. Each mapping is a **function on its own pass** rather than a table inside the settings, so a pass owns its own quality decision and the settings bag stays a bag. The row asked for "a documented table"; there are three, each in the header of the pass it describes, because a single table elsewhere would be a fourth place to keep in step. What the presets are actually worth is measured, not asserted: SSAO's is a real performance dial (3.4× from Low to Ultra), bloom's buys width rather than time (30%), and FXAA's buys look and nothing at all (`MOD-416`, `MOD-528`, `MOD-608`). |
| MOD-728 | `GraphicsDevice::GetRenderPipelineSettingsEXT()` — the accessor the settings doc already claims exists | ⛔ **superseded by `MOD-729`** | `MOD-729` decided the settings live on `RenderPipeline` only: exposing them from `GraphicsDevice` would give an XNA type a member whose type exists only under a compile option. What this row was really about is the doc rot that made it look necessary — `RenderPipelineSettings.hpp` told readers to "construct via `GraphicsDevice::GetRenderPipelineSettings()`", a method that never existed. (The repository's own audit found the same line independently; see `audit/AUDIT_PROGRESS.md`.) The comment now names the two real routes and records why the third is not coming. |
| MOD-729 | Decide whether the settings accessor lives on `GraphicsDevice` (XNA type) or only on `RenderPipeline` | ✅ | Decided: **`RenderPipeline` only**. Exposing the settings from `GraphicsDevice` would give an XNA type a member whose type exists only under a compile option. `RenderPipelineSettings`' own doc comment, which claimed a `GraphicsDevice` accessor, is now wrong and is corrected in MOD-1806. |
| MOD-730 | Settings validation (negative exposure, gamma ≤0, absurd radii) | ✅ **splitting the row's premise** | The row says "clamped, not rejected"; `MOD-22` says "stored, because the passes clamp what they apply". Both are right about different values, and the implementation splits them rather than picking one. **Clamped** are the cases whose out-of-range value is *undefined*: gamma (applied as `pow(x, 1/gamma)`, so zero is a division by zero and the frame comes back infinite), and negative exposure, intensities, radius and FXAA threshold — a sign error rather than a look. **Stored as given** are the merely extreme: a bloom threshold of 100, 500 iterations, 9999 samples. Those have *pass-specific* limits, and a bag that clamped to one pass's range would change the number a caller reads back and force a quality preset to know every pass's limits. Boundaries tested from both sides; `kMinimumGamma` and `kMinimumFxaaEdgeThreshold` are public constants so a test asserts against the same number the implementation uses. |
| MOD-731 | Settings serialization helper (to/from a simple key=value string) for demos and tests | ✅ | `toStringEXT()` / `applyFromStringEXT()`, `key=value;` — every one of the 15 fields round-trips, asserted field by field *and* by writing what was read and comparing the text. Two decisions worth recording. **Unknown keys and malformed values are ignored rather than refused**, which is what lets an older build read a newer settings string and stops one bad field costing a whole look; `applyFromStringEXT` returns how many fields it recognised so a caller that cares can tell. And **every accepted value goes through the ordinary setter**, so a settings file with a stale `gamma=0` in it hits the same floor a call would — asserted, because a loader that wrote members directly is the obvious way to bypass validation. |

### 7.3 Verification

| ID | Task | Status | Acceptance criterion |
|---|---|---|---|
| MOD-735 | Unit tests for every public `RenderPipeline` member (incl. both misuse guards) | ✅ | Done — 13 cases covering both misuse guards, the inert path, format selection, user passes, settings changes, resize and frame-to-frame stability. |
| MOD-736 | Golden image: full pipeline (HDR + bloom + tonemap) on a fixed test scene | ⛔ | No golden, the decision recorded in `MOD-1703`. A committed image of an HDR+bloom+tonemap frame would pin llvmpipe's rasterisation, the exact bloom kernel and the tonemap curve all at once, so any of the three changing legitimately would fail it and the failure would not say which. `cnaext_render_pipeline_test` asserts the properties instead — and one of them is stronger than a golden: turning the whole stack off returns the *bit-identical* frame from before it was turned on, which is an exact comparison against an image the program generated in the same run rather than against one committed months earlier. |
| MOD-737 | Golden image: pipeline with everything off == direct rendering | ✅ | Done — `AnInertPipelineProducesTheSameFrameAsNoPipelineAtAll`: no scene target, no passes, no memory; the frame reaches the back buffer exactly as it would without a pipeline. |
| MOD-738 | 2D-only renderer behavior: pipeline constructs, passes skip, output equals direct rendering | ✅ | Verified by running `cnaext_render_pipeline_test` on Headless, Software and Stub as well as EasyGL. The identity check runs **first and unconditionally**, before any capability gate, because it is what these renderers actually promise: on Software it passes with 0 differing texels, 0 passes and 0 bytes allocated. Headless and Stub SKIP one line later for the reason that is true of them (no readable back buffer), having already run the part that applies. Nothing throws anywhere. |
| MOD-739 | `SpriteBatch` inside `begin/end` works (2D game with HDR bloom) | ✅ — and its first gate was the `MOD-1699` mistake | The 2D half of `cnaext_render_pipeline_test`: an ordinary `SpriteBatch` draw, unchanged, gains bloom by being wrapped — 0 light outside the sprite direct, 639254 through the pipeline. **The gate had to be corrected on first contact with SOFTWARE:** it asked `SupportsCapability(CustomEffects)`, which SOFTWARE answers *true* while executing no shader source, so the program passed the gate and then failed the bloom check three lines later. It now asks the two-part question, and on such a renderer asserts the thing that *is* its contract — that the chain still runs all three passes, each falling back to a copy — before skipping. "The passes were skipped" and "the passes ran and copied" look identical from outside, and only one of them is what was promised. |
| MOD-740 | 3D `Model`/`BasicEffect`/`PbrEffect` inside `begin/end` works | ✅ | A `BasicEffect` `DrawUserPrimitives` inside `begin`/`end`: 388110 total light direct, 351640 through the tonemapper, so the draw reaches the frame *and* the passes act on it. **A detail that cost a debugging round and is now a comment in the source:** the triangle is counter-clockwise in a Y-up NDC, which XNA's default `CullCounterClockwiseFace` removes — so the first run measured a culled triangle and reported zero light from a pipeline that was working perfectly. Not a `Model`: loading content would make this a content test, and `BasicEffect` + `DrawUserPrimitives` is the same effect and the same draw path a `Model` uses. |
| MOD-741 | Example `cnaext_render_pipeline_test` — the canonical demo (3D scene + HDR + bloom + tonemap + FXAA) | ✅ | `modules/graphics-ext/examples/cnaext_render_pipeline_test.cpp`, registered as ctest `CNAEXT_RenderPipeline`; **8/8 checks** on EasyGL. The full stack in one frame — HDR, bloom, tonemap, FXAA — with `getStatistics()` confirming 3 passes, 5 target switches and a scene target in use. The showcase check the others do not make is the **last** one: turning everything back off returns the *identical* frame from check A, so the pipeline holds no state that survives its own settings. |
| MOD-742 | Perf: full-pipeline frame cost vs direct rendering at 720p/1080p per quality preset | ✅ | `cnaext_render_pipeline_test --benchmark`, in `docs/cnaext-perf.md`. At 96×96: direct 0.76 ms, pipeline present but inert **0.84** (+9%, the whole cost of having the object in the frame), and the full stack 3.25 / 3.76 / 4.80 / 4.78 ms for Low / Medium / High / Ultra. The honest reading is in the doc: the stack costing three to four times the scene is an artefact of *this* scene — a sprite and a triangle at 96×96 — because the passes are fullscreen and do not care how little geometry there was. **The engine layer's cost is close to constant per pixel and independent of scene complexity**, which is the opposite of how the scene itself behaves, and that is the shape to plan against. |
| MOD-743 | Leak check under ASan/LSan for 1000 frames + 50 resizes | ✅ | `modules/graphics-ext/examples/cnaext_leak_loop_test.cpp`, ctest `CNAEXT_LeakLoop`: three cycles of **1000 frames + 50 resizes** each — 3000 frames and 150 resizes — with a fresh pipeline per cycle so the destructor path (including `MOD-715`'s `DeviceReset` unsubscribe) is exercised too. Resizes alternate between two sizes so every one is real and the pool must release and reallocate, which is where a leak would accumulate fastest. **Under ASan/LSan the only leak reported is 1032 bytes in `libdbus` via `SDL_DBus_Init`** — an external residual, and the proof it is external is that it is *the same single allocation* after 3000 frames as after the whole test suite: it does not scale with the work. The program also asserts a cheap proxy the ordinary build can check, since ASan is not always available: the memory estimate after 1000 frames must not exceed the estimate after 10. It held at **98304 bytes** across all three cycles. |
| MOD-744 | Thread-safety statement (pipeline is single-threaded, owner-thread only) | ✅ | Documented below with the reason rather than as a rule. The pipeline is **owner-thread only** — construct, configure, `begin`/`end` and destroy on the thread that owns the `GraphicsDevice` — because it is a thin layer over `GraphicsDevice`, `SpriteBatch` and `Effect`, none of which are thread-safe, and a lock here would give a false impression of safety while the calls underneath stayed unsafe. The `DeviceReset` subscription (`MOD-715`) is the one place this could surprise: the handler runs on whichever thread raised the event, which for every renderer today is the same one. |
| MOD-745 | Flip `N20`/`N25` in CNAEXT.md | ✅ | `CNAEXT.md` `N20` and `N25` updated as part of `MOD-23`'s sweep: `N20` ✅ with the note that there is no separate `HdrSceneTarget` type (the pipeline owns its target through `RenderTargetPool`, and a class holding one target would have been a name rather than a boundary), and `N25` 🟨 — the settings-to-pass wiring is done and verified, the per-renderer follow-ups are Phase 16 and open for every renderer but EasyGL. |
