Worth stating plainly that this had never been checked: the helper shipped in `MOD-503`, `SsaoPass` works in UV and depth and never reconstructs a position, and no test drove geometry through the prepass at all. The measurement also produced `MOD-2000`'s fourth finding -- the clear is **white**, not black -- and a second test pins it. `SsaoPass` survives the same clear, but on its **range check** rather than on its zero comparison, which is now asserted rather than left as a coincidence between two unrelated guards. |

### 20.2 Depth of field (`MOD-2010`–`MOD-2016`)

| ID | Task | Status | Acceptance criterion |
|---|---|---|---|
| MOD-2010 | Circle-of-confusion computation from linear depth, focus distance and aperture | ✅ | `DepthOfFieldPass::circleOfConfusionMillimetres` is **public and static**, which is the point: the thin-lens diameter exists twice -- once in C++ so a test can check the optics against the formula, once in GLSL because a shader cannot call the first. Checked at seven depths against the formula computed in the test rather than against recorded numbers, plus both sides of the focus plane, the f-number's direction, and six degenerate configurations that each divide by zero or take the wrong branch of the lens equation. They answer **0**, not infinity: there is no image, so there is no circle -- and a NaN in the blur radius would silently disable the whole pass. |
| MOD-2011 | `DepthOfFieldPass`: near/far separable blur weighted by CoC | ✅ | A sixteen-tap golden-angle disc rather than a separable pair, because the acceptance test below is per-tap and a separable blur cannot ask it. Verified on a frame split into a near and a far half, each carrying a checkerboard -- **contrast is what a blur destroys, so contrast is what is measured**; a flat colour blurs into itself and shows nothing. Moving the focus moves which half is sharp, which is the anti-vacuity partner: a pass that did nothing would leave the near half sharp whatever the settings said. In the pipeline it sits **before bloom**, so an out-of-focus highlight blooms as the spread circle it became rather than the point it was. |
| MOD-2012 | Bleed control: in-focus pixels must not smear onto out-of-focus neighbours | ✅ | A tap contributes only if its **own** circle of confusion is wide enough to reach the pixel gathering it -- which is what scattering light onto a neighbour actually means, and is the whole difference between depth of field and a depth-weighted blur. Tested with a white sharp half against a black blurred one, reading the rows two past the depth step: without the rule a 0.06 gather reaches four rows and those rows carry a substantial share of the white. |
| MOD-2013 | Settings wiring (enable, focus distance, focal length, aperture, max CoC) | ✅ | Five fields, off by default. The bag wins over the pass's own values, asserted by telling the pass to focus near and the bag to focus far and checking which half came back sharp. A zero or negative lens is refused rather than clamped -- it is nonsense, not an out-of-range value with an obvious meaning -- while `maxRadius` is clamped, because it is a budget with a well-defined ceiling. |
| MOD-2014 | Tests and `isSupported()` | ✅ | **14 tests.** The one worth naming is `TheShaderMatchesTheCpuReference`, which pins the two copies of the formula together without a debug output: `maxRadius` caps whatever the shader computed, so raising it stops changing the frame exactly where it passes the shader's own radius, and that point is checked against the CPU function. Below it the frame is measurably sharper; at and above it the frame stops changing. Plus both copy-through cases -- no depth image, and no camera, since the far plane is what turns a normalised depth back into metres. |
| MOD-2015 | Documentation | ✅ | A section in the parameters' own terms, leading with the assumption hiding inside them -- **one world unit is one metre** -- and with the thing most likely to be mistaken for a bug: the same optics that make f/1.4 dramatic on a camera put a 50 mm lens's background circle at 0.7% of the frame, fifteen pixels at 1080p and under half a pixel at 64. A game seeing "no blur" should check its frame size before its f-number, and `circleOfConfusionMillimetres` is public so the number can be computed rather than guessed. |

### 20.3 Lens and grading passes (`MOD-2020`–`MOD-2029`)

The cheapest group: each is one fullscreen pass with no new inputs.

| ID | Task | Status | Acceptance criterion |
|---|---|---|---|
| MOD-2020 | `ColorGradePass`: 3D LUT applied as a 2D strip texture | ✅ | A strip of N slices of N by N -- the layout every grading tool exports to, and the only one a renderer without 3D textures can sample. Blue is interpolated by hand between two slice lookups because a strip has no filtering across slices (the neighbouring slice is half a table away in u); red and green ride the sampler's own bilinear filtering inside a slice. **The test that catches the most is the dullest one:** an identity table must reproduce the frame. A strip lookup is off-by-half-a-texel arithmetic throughout and every mistake in it produces a frame that still looks like a frame -- slightly washed, slightly shifted, entirely plausible. Its counterpart is a table that swaps red and blue, which is what proves the lookup is read at all and read in the right order. Placed **after tonemapping and before FXAA**: a table indexed by scene-referred values would be asked about numbers past 1.0 where it has nothing to say, and the edge filter should see the contrast the viewer will see, since a grade that lifts the blacks changes which edges there are to find. |
| MOD-2021 | LUT loading and validation (size, strip layout, sRGB flag) | ✅ | `lutSizeForStrip(width, height)` states the rule once and answers 0 for anything that is not a strip; `setLut` refuses those **by name rather than sampling them**, because a strip read at the wrong slice count grades the frame into colours nothing in the table names -- a wrong image that looks deliberate. A square texture, an off-by-one width, a single entry and a size past the maximum are each tested, since all four are textures somebody might reasonably hand the pass. `createIdentityLut` gives a caller something correct to start from and validates its own ends. **No sRGB flag:** the pass runs after tonemapping on displayed pixels, so there is one encoding and nothing to choose between; adding a flag would have been a setting with one correct value. |
| MOD-2022 | `ChromaticAberrationPass`: per-channel radial offset | ✅ | Red sampled further out, blue further in, scaled by distance from the axis -- so the centre stays sharp however strong the setting is, which is what makes it read as a lens rather than as a bug. **The first version of its test measured nothing**: a single vertical edge down the middle of the frame sits exactly where the radial offset has no horizontal component. The scene is stripes across the whole frame now, which gives the sampler something to separate at every radius, and the test asserts both halves of the claim -- flat at the axis, strongly fringed near the edge. |
| MOD-2023 | `FilmGrainPass`: time-varying noise, luminance-weighted | ✅ | The weight is what separates grain from noise: buried in blacks, invisible in blown highlights, all of it in the midtones where an emulsion actually responds. Asserted as a ratio between two halves of one frame. Seeded by the pixel and `elapsedSeconds` and nothing else, so it is **deterministic** -- the same time produces the same grain, pixel for pixel, which is what makes a rendered sequence reproducible; a separate assertion checks it does move between different times, since a constant would satisfy the first test perfectly. |
| MOD-2024 | `LensFlarePass`: threshold, ghosts along the centre vector, halo | 🟨 | Ghosts and threshold done; **no halo**. The ghosts are the part with a testable claim -- a bright block in one quadrant must brighten the opposite one and leave its own side alone, which is what a reflection between lens elements does and is the one property that makes flare read as a lens rather than a smear. The halo is a radial blur of the same thresholded image and adds a second look without a second claim, so it is left rather than written untested. Four ghosts, brightness falling with index. |
| MOD-2025 | Settings wiring for all four | ✅ | Seven fields across the four passes, and **every one of them off by default** -- a strength or intensity of zero disables its pass outright rather than running it with no effect, so a game that did not opt in pays nothing and sees nothing. Each pass reads the bag in preference to its own value. |
| MOD-2026 | Tests for all four | ✅ | **19 tests.** The first thing each of the four is asked is whether it leaves an untouched frame untouched, which is not a formality: four passes added to a chain a game did not opt into is exactly how a "no visual change" release stops being one. Beyond that, the identity LUT (the dullest test here and the one that catches the most), the channel-swap table that proves the lookup is read in the right order, radial-not-constant aberration, grain determinism and its midtone weighting, and the ghosts' side of the frame. |
| MOD-2027 | Documentation | ✅ | A section built around the question that decides all four positions: is this describing the lens the scene was shot through, or the image the viewer is looking at? Flare and bloom answer the first and run before tonemapping; grade, aberration and grain answer the second and run after -- with grain last of all, because an edge filter handed fresh noise spends its budget smoothing the grain instead of the edges. |

### 20.4 Motion blur and the velocity buffer (`MOD-2030`–`MOD-2037`)

| ID | Task | Status | Acceptance criterion |
|---|---|---|---|
| MOD-2030 | Decide: camera-only reprojection first, per-object velocity second | ✅ | **Camera-only first, and the two are not the same size of change.** Camera reprojection needs one extra matrix and no new render target: the depth image and the camera give a world position, and the previous frame's camera says where that position was on screen. Per-object velocity needs a third prepass output *and* a previous world matrix supplied per draw -- a contract change on the application, which has to remember last frame's transform for every object it draws. The first is a pass; the second is a new obligation on every game that wants it. Splitting them means camera blur ships now and the obligation is proposed on its own, in `MOD-2033`, rather than smuggled in as part of a pass. |
| MOD-2031 | Previous-frame view/projection carried in `PostProcessContext` | ✅ | Three fields: `inverseView` (a cross-frame comparison has to happen somewhere that did not move, and view space moved with the camera), `previousViewProjection`, and `hasPreviousFrame` -- the third being the one that is easy to leave out, since an identity matrix is a perfectly convincing previous camera. **The history advances in `end()` and nowhere else**, which is its own test: advancing it in `setCamera` would make a game that sets the camera twice in a frame compare against a camera nothing was drawn with, and the blur would be wrong in a way no single frame could reveal. |
| MOD-2032 | `MotionBlurPass`: reconstruct velocity from depth + reprojection, blur along it | ✅ | Eight samples walked *backwards* along the velocity, which is where the pixel came from and what a shutter open across the movement records. The sky is skipped rather than reprojected: it is infinitely far, so it does not move with the camera's translation and reprojecting it through a rotation would be right only by accident. Tested with vertical stripes, which are pure horizontal contrast: a sideways pan destroys them and a still camera must not. `maxDistance` caps the smear -- not a look but a hitch guard, since one slow frame makes every velocity enormous and a single stutter would otherwise smear the whole image. |
| MOD-2033 | Per-object velocity as a third prepass target | ✅ | Delivered as what it always was — **an obligation on the application, made expressible**. `DepthNormalPrepass::setVelocityEnabledEXT` is **off by default**, and the default is the feature: with it off nothing changes, and with it on an app must supply `setPreviousWorldEXT` per draw and `setPreviousCameraEXT` per frame. An app that turns it on and supplies neither gets an image saying everything is stationary — which is what it already had, so nothing breaks and nothing is gained. The cost `MOD-2030` refused to pay is paid explicitly and measured by a test: with MRT the prepass binds three targets in one pass; **without MRT its two-pass fallback becomes three**, and `TurningItOnAddsATargetAndOnlyThenAPass` asserts the count moves only when velocity is on and moves back when it is off. The MRT verdict is re-probed on the switch rather than assumed, because a renderer that binds two targets is not promising three. Two encoding decisions recorded where they were made. **Alpha is inverted** — a texel *below* 0.5 carries a velocity — because the MRT path issues one clear for the whole bound set and depth must clear to white, so a shared white clear already writes "nothing here"; a second clear would mean binding a discard-contents target, which is not safe. And both clip positions cross to the fragment stage **undivided**: the perspective divide is not affine, so interpolating divided values would put a large triangle's velocity in the wrong place everywhere except at its vertices. `MotionBlurPass` consumes it as a **per-pixel upgrade, not a second mode**: the stored velocity already contains the camera's contribution, so it replaces the reconstruction where present, and every pixel the velocity image does not cover — the sky, any object drawn without a previous world — still takes the camera path. `PostProcessContext::sourceVelocity` and `RenderPipeline::setVelocityInputEXT` carry it, both defaulting to null. 7 cases, and the camera is held **identical in both frames** throughout, which removes the camera-reprojection path from every answer: with a static camera the old blur has nothing to compute, so any smear that appears is the object's. That is asserted in both directions — the smear appears, *and* the camera-only run is pixel-for-pixel the unblurred source. A stationary object records exactly zero; an object that moved left-to-right records +X and **nothing in Y**, which is what a transposed matrix or a swapped component would break while still producing a plausible smear; and two displacements four times apart record different magnitudes, which a constant could not. One limitation stated rather than faked: a **skinned** mesh's velocity is its object's motion, not its deformation. The previous pose is not reconstructed, because that needs the previous frame's whole bone set as a second array — an obligation an order of magnitude larger than one matrix.  **Follow-up from `MOD-2035` (2026-08-20):** the velocity encoding was written in the prepass's shader and read back in `MotionBlurPass`'s — two copies of a scaled UV delta with an *inverted* alpha flag, which is exactly the shape that had just cost this phase a latent SSAO bug. It is single-sourced now through `DepthNormalPrepass::getVelocityDecodeGlsl()`, which emits `cnaEncodeVelocity`/`cnaNoVelocity`/`cnaHasVelocity`/`cnaDecodeVelocity` for both ends, plus CPU twins (`hasVelocityEXT`, `decodeVelocityEXT`). The tests decode through the twins rather than reading bytes, which makes them an agreement test as a side effect: the image is written by the shader and read by the C++, so a twin that disagreed would fail them. |
| MOD-2035 | `CNAEXT_Showcase` check E measures nothing: SSAO contributes zero to that frame | ✅ | **Closed, after one wrong closure that this row records rather than deletes.** Earlier today this row was marked done on the strength of a measurement showing the format bisection no longer reproduced. That measurement did not survive: it reverted on the same machine with the same code, and re-running it repeatedly gives the original result. **The bisection recorded here for several sessions was right.** The lesson is the one this phase keeps re-learning — a measurement that contradicts four earlier ones is a reason to measure again, not a reason to close a row. The fix is to stop using the format that fails. `DepthNormalPrepass` now packs depth into an 8-bit target on **every** renderer, and the numbers are unambiguous: SSAO driven from the real prepass occludes **0 pixels of 16384 with half-float depth and 2101 with packed**, and `CNAEXT_Showcase` check E goes from **0 strongly-occluded pixels to 1022**. Packing is not a workaround with a cost — this class's own documentation already called it the *more* precise encoding (1 part in 2^24 against a half-float's 11-bit mantissa) and it needs no capability at all, so it removes a per-renderer branch rather than adding one. **The mechanism is still not established, and the code says so.** `HalfFloatDepthSamplingTests` is the attempt to reduce it: two textures *proven* to hold identical values, one half-float and one 8-bit, and the same loop inlined over each — run twice, once with no depth attachment and once with the `Depth24` the prepass actually allocates, because a reduction that does not build the failing shape is not a reduction. Both shapes agree, so **three attributes are now ruled out as the variable** — the format alone, the depth attachment, and the loop itself — leaving the difference in what the real pass does that the reduction does not: perspective geometry through an MRT prepass, a sample coordinate computed from two other texture reads, and a 64-entry uniform array. That list is in the test file so the next attempt starts where this one stopped. The test is kept for the guard it can still provide (a renderer that ever fails that comparison must not be storing depth in the failing format) and it prints its finding rather than asserting a driver defect nobody has isolated. Two real bugs were found on the way and both are fixed. **`SsaoPass` read the depth channel raw** (`texture(...).r`) instead of decoding it, so on every renderer without half-float render targets it had been comparing the top byte of a packed value against itself and calling the result occlusion — a plausible frame, which is why nothing caught it. And the packed/half-float decision was **re-derived independently in seven places**; it is now single-sourced through `DepthNormalPrepass::usesPackedDepthEXT`, without which this change would have left six passes decoding an encoding the prepass had stopped writing. The showcase fix from the wrong closure stands on its own and is unaffected: it drove the prepass with `drawScene()`, whose every draw calls `Apply()` on the scene's own effects and replaced the prepass program, so the depth target held the shaded frame's red channel. Both defects were real and independent; only one of them was the whole story. 17 tests across eight files hand-built depth images in the old encoding and now build the current one through one shared helper (`CnaTest::EngineLayer::DepthTexel`), which is the same single-sourcing applied to the tests. |
| MOD-2034 | Settings, tests, documentation | ✅ | Two settings, off by default. **Seven tests**, each changing exactly one thing between two frames of the same scene, because a blur that reacted to nothing -- or to everything -- would still produce a blurred frame: still camera, panning camera, no history, the cap biting, missing depth, the accessors, and the pipeline's own history advancing once per frame rather than once per `setCamera`. The documentation leads with the boundary rather than the feature. |

### 20.5 Many lights: clustered forward (`MOD-2040`–`MOD-2049`)

Today the forward shader carries three directional lights plus one punctual light with its shadow.
"Modern" here means hundreds.

| ID | Task | Status | Acceptance criterion |
|---|---|---|---|
| MOD-2040 | Decide the shape: clustered forward (`Forward+`) rather than deferred, with the reason | ✅ | **Clustered forward.** The argument against deferred is not taste, it is what this layer already had to build: `DepthNormalPrepass` needs *two* targets and still carries a hand-written two-pass fallback, because `MultipleRenderTargets` is a capability some renderers advertise and then refuse in practice — the fallback exists because WebGPU is one of them, and it is probed by doing rather than by asking. A G-buffer wants three or four targets, so on any renderer without MRT the same fallback becomes three or four full geometry passes, and the scene is drawn four times to save iterating a light list. Clustered forward inverts that trade: geometry is drawn once, the existing `PbrEffect`/`SkinnedPbrEffect` keep their material code, and what is added is a per-cluster light index list the fragment shader walks. The costs are stated rather than hidden — the light list has to reach the shader through a storage buffer *or a texture* (`MOD-2044`), because GL ES 3.0 has no storage buffers; transparency is still forward-lit and therefore still works, which deferred would have cost; and MSAA keeps working, which deferred would also have cost. What clustered does not buy is deferred`s decoupling of shading from geometry complexity, so a scene with heavy overdraw shades more than once. That is the trade, and it is accepted here because the alternative`s worst case is bounded by a capability this layer has already been burned by. |
| MOD-2041 | View-frustum cluster grid (16×8×24) and its bounds | ✅ | `ClusteredLightGrid` is the geometry half of clustered forward and holds no lights and no device resources. **Slices are spaced exponentially**: every slice has the same ratio of far to near distance, so the near clusters -- where geometry is dense and lights are close -- are the thin ones, where even spacing would put almost every slice past the middle distance in clusters large enough to hold the whole scene. The cluster index runs x fastest so a depth slice is contiguous, and that order is API rather than a detail, because the fragment shader has to reproduce it. **The reference the bounds are checked against contains no matrices**: it is built from `tan(fov/2)` and the aspect ratio, so a convention error in the unprojection would show up as disagreement rather than as two copies of the same mistake -- and it caught that XNA`s projection puts the near plane at NDC z **0**, not −1. The bounds are computed by unprojecting each tile corner at both NDC depths and interpolating in view space rather than by scaling a ray by `1/z`; that form is linear for an orthographic projection too, which is why one falls out and is tested. **One assertion in the first draft of the tests was simply false and is recorded rather than quietly deleted**: it asked neighbouring clusters to share a face. They share a frustum *plane*, not an axis-aligned one -- along a tile edge left of centre a box reaches furthest left at the far distance and furthest right at the near one, so adjacent boxes overlap in a staircase. Overlap is correct for a conservative bound; the property that matters is that there is never a *gap*, which would drop a light sitting between two clusters, and that is what is asserted now, together with every point landing inside the box of the cluster the tile arithmetic names for it. |
| MOD-2042 | Light assignment to clusters on the CPU | ✅ | `ClusteredLightAssignment` sorts world-space light spheres into a grid`s clusters and stores the result the way the GPU will want it: **one flat index array plus one offset per cluster, with a spare offset at the end**, so a cluster`s count is the difference between neighbouring offsets and no second array is needed. A shader cannot follow a pointer, so contiguity is not an optimisation here, it is the format. The test is a sphere against the cluster`s axis-aligned bounds, which is **conservative twice over** -- the bounds are already a box around a frustum shape and the sphere is already a box around a light`s reach -- and both errors point the same way: a light that only nearly touches costs one iteration of the shader`s light loop, while a light that is dropped is a hole in the lighting. The search is narrowed by depth before it starts, since a sphere spans a known range of view distances and most of the twenty-four slices are never visited; the screen axes are scanned in full inside those slices, because a light behind the camera or straddling the near plane has no honest screen-space extent to narrow by. **The narrowing is exactly the kind of optimisation that silently loses a light at a boundary**, so the reference in the tests visits every cluster with no narrowing at all and the two results must be identical -- checked on a single light, forty scattered ones under a moved camera, lights placed *on* every slice boundary and a thousandth either side of it, and the four ways a light can be outside the frustum. Two smaller decisions are pinned because a shader depends on them: indices within a cluster are sorted and never repeat, since the shader walks the list without deduplicating and a repeat would double a light`s contribution -- which reads as a brightness tuning problem rather than a bug; and a zero-radius light is carried rather than removed, so switching a light off by setting its range to zero does not renumber the lights after it. |
| MOD-2043 | GPU light assignment via compute, where compute is available | ✅ | `ClusteredLightCompute` runs the same sort in a compute shader and **the assignment it produces is element-for-element the one the CPU builds**, which is the only claim worth making: everything downstream refers to a light by index, so "close enough" would mean lighting a different object. That equality is bought by choosing the naive shape on purpose -- one invocation owns one cluster and walks every light in order, with no atomics, no shared memory and no two-pass compaction -- so a cluster`s list comes out sorted for free and nothing races because no two invocations write to the same place. Two details make the equality hold rather than nearly hold: the spheres are transformed into view space on the CPU, since a view matrix is the one input that has nothing to do with the grid`s shape; and the shader is handed **the grid`s own inverse projection** rather than inverting the same projection again, because two inversions of one matrix differ in the last bits and that is enough to move a light sitting on a cluster boundary -- which would make the equality false for a reason belonging to neither path. Verified on this machine`s real compute path, not skipped: one light, forty scattered under a moved camera, the four ways a light can be outside the frustum, a zero-radius light, and no lights at all. **It falls back rather than refuses** -- no compute, or a device that advertises compute and then rejects the program, runs the CPU path and says so through `usedCompute()` and a reason string, because a frame with its lights unsorted is a worse answer than a frame sorted more slowly. The per-cluster capacity is fixed, since a GPU cannot grow an array; a cluster past it keeps the first `stride` lights and raises `hasOverflowed()`, a number a game can act on rather than a silent truncation. `ClusteredLightAssignment::adopt` is the one door into an assignment that did not build itself, and it validates rather than trusts. |
| MOD-2044 | Light list upload: storage buffer where available, texture otherwise | 🟨 | **The texture path is built and verified on the GPU; the storage-buffer path is deliberately not built, and the reason is the bound.** `ClusteredLightBuffer` uploads three textures -- the light data at sixteen texels per light, the cluster table at two texels per cluster (where its list starts, how long it is), and the index list at one texel per reference. **Every value is stored as the four bytes of its IEEE representation in an ordinary `Color` texture** and read back with `texelFetch` and `uintBitsToFloat`. That is forced rather than chosen: this renderer`s textures are 8-bit only (`Task 176`), so a float cannot be stored as one; a normalised encoding would need a range decided in advance and would lose precision wherever the range was generous; and uniform arrays cannot hold 256 lights inside GL ES 3.0`s uniform limits. `texelFetch` is what makes the byte encoding exact -- integer coordinates, no filtering, no rounding -- and **that exactness is the claim the tests actually check on the GPU**, with a shader that decodes each light, compares every field against uniforms holding the same numbers and paints the answer, on values picked to be awkward rather than round. A second test feeds the probe a deliberately *wrong* position, because a shader that paints white on agreement proves nothing until it has been seen to paint black. The storage-buffer variant is refused for a stated reason: an SSBO in a **fragment** shader needs GLSL ES 3.10, and this layer`s shader floor is 3.00 by `MOD-15`; a second shader profile for the whole light-loop path is real cost with no measurement saying the texture path is the bottleneck. The storage buffer stays where 3.10 already applies -- the compute side, `MOD-2043`. `kMaxLights` being 256 is not arbitrary either: a light index then fits in one byte, which is why the index list can afford a texel per reference. |
| MOD-2045 | Clustered shading: a fragment walks its cluster's light list | 🟨 | **Delivered as `ClusteredForwardEffect`, not as a change to `PbrEffect`, and the reason is structural rather than stylistic.** `PbrEffect` owns no shader source at all: it fills a `GpuDrawParams` and the *renderer* generates the program. Teaching it a light loop therefore means changing EasyGL`s built-in effect family -- code compiled into every game whether `CNA_CNAEXT` is on or off -- which is precisely what the guard rule exists to prevent, and the row`s original wording had not noticed that. So the engine layer gets its own `ShaderEffect`-based PBR effect instead, in the layer, behind the guard, with the same vertex format as `DepthNormalPrepass`. What a game gives up by using it is `PbrEffect`s texture set and its one shadowed punctual light; what it gains is the light count. The reflectance is the GGX / Smith / Schlick trio, and the falloff is glTF`s **windowed** inverse square -- exactly zero at the range rather than asymptotic, because the light was sorted into clusters by a sphere of that radius and anything it still contributed outside would be light no cluster knows about. **The acceptance criterion is met and then some**: 256 lights render, and a `contribution()` mirror of the shader runs on the CPU so the test compares the centre pixel against a *number* rather than against "brighter than before" -- the two agree to within 0.03 of a normalised channel. The counterpart test moves the light behind the camera and requires the wall to come back exactly black, then moves it back and requires it not to, because a shader ignoring the cluster list entirely would pass the first half. Roughness has a floor of 0.04 for a stated reason: a perfectly smooth distribution divides by zero in the specular term. The bound in the status is the second half of the original criterion -- there is no "identical frame to the existing single-light path", because that path belongs to a different effect with a different BRDF, and claiming parity between them would be a claim nothing tests. |
| MOD-2046 | `ClusteredLightSetEXT`: the app-facing collection | ✅ | Taken ahead of `MOD-2043`–`MOD-2045` because all three refer to a light by its index, and the index has to be handed out somewhere first. `PointLightEXT` and `SpotLightEXT` stay exactly as they are; the set converts them into one uniform `ClusteredLightEXT` record, because a clustered light list gives every light one index in one array and an array cannot hold two structures -- so a light`s type becomes a field, and a game keeps writing the specific type it means. **Adding validates rather than accepting and skipping**: a non-positive range, a negative intensity, a cone whose inner angle exceeds its outer or reaches past a hemisphere, a zero direction, or any non-finite number is refused where it is added, because a light that silently does nothing is far harder to find than one that refused to be added. Zero *intensity* is deliberately allowed and is the supported way to switch a light off, since `removeAt` renumbers everything after it while `replaceAt` does not. The maximum is 256 and is refused rather than grown. **The bounding volume is the part that had to be got right**: a point light`s is its range, but a spot`s is a cone`s bounding sphere -- two cases either side of 45 degrees, and *not centred on the light*, which is what keeps a torch out of every cluster behind the person holding it, a 0.15 rad cone needing barely half the radius the naive sphere would. The lit volume is a spherical sector, so the tests sample the curved end as well as the sides: a cone with a flat base would leave the bulge outside the sphere, and nothing else here would notice. **Renamed after the fact, and the reason is worth the row**: this was first written as `PunctualLightSetEXT` holding a `PunctualLightEXT`, which collides with the existing `Microsoft::Xna::Framework::Graphics::PunctualLightEXT` -- a genuinely different thing, the *one* shadowed punctual light a lit effect receives per draw, whose budget of one is a deliberate ceiling (`MOD-1007`). Two types with the same name in two namespaces describing different concepts is exactly the confusion this project`s naming rules exist to prevent, so the list member became `ClusteredLightEXT` and sits with the rest of the `Clustered*` family. |
| MOD-2047 | Shadow policy for many lights | ✅ | **The honest statement first, because it is the substance of the row: clustered shading removed the limit on how many lights can *light* a scene and removed nothing at all about how many can *shadow* one.** A shadow map is a render target and a geometry pass -- six of them for a point light -- so two hundred shadowed lights would draw the geometry twelve hundred times before shading a pixel. Shadows therefore stay a small budget while lighting does not, and `ClusteredShadowPolicyEXT` is where that budget is spent rather than a place where the problem disappears. The rule is one sentence: **among the lights that asked for a shadow and are visible, the budget goes to the ones contributing most light at the camera** -- scored with the same windowed falloff `ClusteredForwardEffect` shades with, so the ranking and the picture agree about which light is brightest, and weighted by Rec. 709 luminance because a green lamp and a blue one of equal numeric intensity do not carry equal weight in an image. A light whose volume misses the frustum scores zero rather than being removed, so `getScore` can still explain why it lost, and `getRefusedCount` is the number worth logging. **Selection is sticky**, because two lights whose scores cross every few frames would otherwise trade the map back and forth and the shadow would blink -- which reads as a bug in the shadow system rather than as a budget working. The stickiness is applied as a *bonus to the incumbent`s score* rather than as an "unless the challenger beats it by X" clause in the comparator: that clause is not transitive, and a sort given an intransitive comparator is entitled to do anything at all. Ties are broken by index through a stable sort, and a test runs five identical frames to say so. |
| MOD-2048 | Tests, perf measurement, documentation | ✅ | Closes 20.5. `cna_test_cnaext_clustered_lights` runs the whole path end to end as a ctest and reports its cost per light count under `--benchmark`; the numbers and their recipe are in `docs/cnaext-perf.md`, and the capability boundary is a new section of `docs/cnaext-engine-layer.md`. **The measurement earned its keep three times over.** It found that the CPU sort recomputed a cluster`s bounds *once per light*, so the frame`s most expensive arithmetic was multiplied by the light count -- 73 ms for 256 lights, and 3.2 ms once the bounds were cached lazily inside one `assign`. It found that timing a draw call measures *submission*: GL is asynchronous and the first attempt reported 0.06 ms for work that had not happened, so the frame is now read back inside the timed block and the read-back`s own cost measured separately and subtracted. And it found that without discarded warm-up frames the one-light row came out an order of magnitude **slower** than the sixteen-light one, which was texture upload and pipeline state rather than shading. The result the numbers actually show: **the GPU sort is flat in the light count and the CPU sort is not** -- about 2.4 ms either way on the GPU because every cluster is visited regardless -- so the two cross between 128 and 256 lights here, and below that the CPU path is the better choice rather than a fallback. Shading dominates past a handful of lights and scales with *lights per fragment*, not lights in the scene; the benchmark deliberately uses the arrangement that defeats clustering -- all 256 lights packed against a wall that fills the frame, 69 in the busiest cluster -- so the figure is a worst case rather than a flattering one. Full suite after the section: 8121 run, 8057 passed, the rest skipped, `CNAEXT_Showcase` still the one red gate (`MOD-2035`). |

### 20.6 Volumetrics (`MOD-2050`–`MOD-2057`)

| ID | Task | Status | Acceptance criterion |
|---|---|---|---|
| MOD-2050 | `HeightFogPass`: analytic exponential height fog from depth | ✅ | **Not a distance fade with a height term attached.** The fog is a medium whose density falls off with height, and what reaches the camera is that density *integrated along the view ray* -- so a valley fills while the hilltop stays clear, and looking down through the valley fogs correctly because the integral knows the ray passed through the thick part. The integral has a closed form, so there is no marching and no step count to tune; `opticalDepth` is public and checked against the formula at 36 combinations of height, ray climb and distance. **A level look is written out separately rather than nudged**: the general form divides by the ray's climb, and pushing that away from zero would make a level view's fog depend on the size of the nudge -- a constant nobody chose. Four degenerate configurations answer 0, which keeps a NaN out of the mix factor where it would turn the whole frame into fog colour rather than fail. Placed first among the scene-referred passes, before motion blur: fog is part of what the shutter collected, so a moving camera should smear the fogged image rather than fog a smeared one. |
| MOD-2051 | `LightShaftPass`: radial blur from a screen-space light position | ✅ | **A shaft is the shape of an occluder, not a glow**, and getting the test to say that took two corrections worth recording. The first scene put a black bar on a black background: a walk gathers *brightness*, so a dark occluder on darkness subtracts nothing and both sides came back identical. An occluder has to take light away, so it is now a cut-out in the lit region. The second was the render-target flip (`MOD-2000`) — the images are built in the coordinates the pass *samples* them in, or a light placed at the top of the frame lands at the bottom of what the shader sees, which is the trap this plan already documented and I walked into anyway. **The light's screen position is the app's to supply**: the layer does not know which of a scene's lights is the sun, and it is a line of arithmetic the app has the matrices for. Positions outside [0, 1] are meaningful and not clamped — a light just past the edge still throws shafts inward, and the effect fades with how far outside rather than cutting off at the border. Placed before fog: shafts are light travelling through air, so the fog that dims distance should dim them too. |
| MOD-2052 | Froxel volumetric scattering (3D volume march) | ✅ | **Done, and the storage decision is the substance of the row.** CNA has a `Texture3D` a shader can *sample* and no render target that writes into one, and filling a volume with compute needs the image stores GL ES refuses for CNA's textures (`MOD-1514`). So the volume is a **slice atlas** -- a 2D target holding the depth slices side by side, the layout `ColorGradePass` already reads a 3D lookup table from -- and one fullscreen draw fills every slice, because each atlas pixel knows which froxel it is and can march to it alone. Slices are spaced quadratically so the near ones are thinner, keeping the resolution where the eye is. **What makes it worth its cost over `HeightFogPass` is the shadow map**: without one the medium is lit wherever the light points, which is a haze rather than a beam, so the test that matters puts a map in and takes it out with nothing else changed. Two of my own mistakes are recorded because both produced plausible frames: the phase function's angle was negated, computing the *backward* lobe and making a view into the sun the darkest thing in the scene; and the first tests asserted the frame got brighter, which is the wrong question to ask of fog -- a dense medium extinguishes a lit source faster than it scatters into it, so the scenes are black and everything that comes back is light the medium put there. |
| MOD-2053 | Atmospheric scattering sky model as a `Skybox` alternative | ✅ | **A time of day becomes a number instead of an asset.** `AtmosphericSky` computes single-scattered Rayleigh and Mie radiance per view ray from the sun direction and a turbidity, so the blue of noon, the white glare around the sun and the orange of a low sun all fall out of the same three lines rather than being three settings. The cube path is untouched; this is an alternative to `Skybox`, not a replacement, because a cube map can hold a painting and this can only hold a sky. The model is exposed as a **public static `radiance()` that mirrors the shader exactly**, so the physics is checked as ratios between channels and directions on the CPU rather than against a screenshot -- and so a game can ask what colour the sky is for an ambient or fog term without rendering one. **The first model was wrong in a way that still drew a convincing sky**, and it is the substance of this row: it summed the view path and the sun path into one extinction, `(1 - exp(-tau_view - tau_sun))`, which saturates -- so a longer path made the sky *dimmer*, the horizon came back darker than the zenith and the sunset came back bluer than noon. The two lengths do opposite jobs and must stay apart: the view path is how much lit air is being looked through, so more of it is brighter, while the sun path is what the light lost getting in, so more of it is dimmer and redder. A sunset is that second term. Compounding it, `cnaAirMass` fed a *cosine* into a fit whose argument is a zenith angle in **degrees**, so the air mass ran from 0.88 overhead to 1.16 at the horizon instead of 1 to 38 -- path length had almost no effect at all. Both are now Kasten and Young`s fit on the real angle, and the coefficients are stated as optical depth through one vertical column so nothing has to carry metres. **Two of the tests were also wrong, in the opposite direction, and were corrected rather than the model bent to fit them**: they placed the sun at the zenith and then asked the zenith to be blue and dimmer than the horizon -- but a camera looking at the zenith under an overhead sun is looking straight *at* the sun, where both phase functions peak and every real photograph is white, so the sun moved to 45 degrees where the question means what it says; and `LookingTowardsTheSunIsBrighterThanLookingAway` had the two directions swapped, because a sun *direction* of -Z puts the sun at +Z. Turbidity 1 means air with no aerosol in it, matching what the word denotes, so the Mie term scales with `turbidity - 1` and vanishes there. |
| MOD-2054 | Settings, tests, documentation | ✅ | Closes 20.6. The seven volumetrics fields were already on `RenderPipelineSettings` and already read by the three passes, but **nothing had ever asserted their defaults**, so this adds the two tests that pin them: each pass is off because *its own amount* is zero -- density, density, intensity -- rather than because of an enable flag, so the zero is the switch and it is what a regression would break silently. The other four fields are shapes rather than amounts (falloff, base height, threshold, decay), so their defaults are usable values and a `-1` lands on the value that means off rather than being stored and inverted downstream; decay is clamped in *both* directions because above 1 a per-step multiplier brightens with distance and diverges. The per-pass "zero leaves the frame alone" tests already existed and were left where they are, next to the pass they constrain. Documentation adds two sections to `docs/cnaext-engine-layer.md`: one comparing the three volumetrics passes as different costs answering different questions rather than as alternatives, and one on `AtmosphericSky` -- including the two things that produced convincing but wrong frames here, the summed extinction and the aureole. Full suite after the section: 8049 run, 7985 passed, the rest skipped, `CNAEXT_Showcase` still the one red gate (`MOD-2035`). |

### 20.7 Area lights (`MOD-2060`–`MOD-2064`)

| ID | Task | Status | Acceptance criterion |
|---|---|---|---|
| MOD-2060 | `AreaLightEXT`: rectangle, disc and tube, in the XNA namespace beside `PunctualLightEXT` | ✅ | The third kind of light, after directional and punctual: one that is a *surface*, which is what almost every real light is. The difference is not brightness but shape -- a window is a bright rectangle in a polished floor, and no amount of tuning turns a point light into one. Placed in the XNA namespace beside `ImageBasedLightEXT` and `PunctualLightEXT` for the reason `MOD-1222` settled: an always-compiled XNA header must not include one that exists only under `CNA_CNAEXT`, and an effect`s public surface must not depend on a build flag. **The shape is a centre and two half-axes rather than four corners**, so all three shapes share one description and none of them can be handed a non-planar or self-intersecting outline. `IsValidEXT` refuses the case that is not obviously degenerate: two axes that both have length but lie along the same line enclose no area, and the form factor answers that with a division by zero rather than with darkness -- while a *tube* is a line with a radius rather than a surface, so parallel axes are meaningful there and are accepted. The absence of area-light shadows is written into the header rather than left for `MOD-2063`, because the failure looks like a bug in the shadow system when it is met without warning. |
| MOD-2061 | Linearly-transformed-cosine tables, generated rather than shipped | 🟨 | **The fitted LTC matrix is not generated, and the reason is arithmetic rather than preference.** That table is the output of a Nelder–Mead fit run per cell: at the published 64×64 resolution, with the order of forty iterations each evaluating a 32×32 sample set, it is upwards of a hundred million BRDF evaluations -- seconds to minutes at every game start. It is a build-time artefact, and this layer has no asset path to ship one through, which is the same constraint that made `MOD-610` refuse SMAA. So the row is delivered as the half that importance sampling can produce cheaply *and exactly*: `AreaLightBrdfTable` generates the BRDF`s **directional albedo**, its **Fresnel weight**, and its **average reflection direction**, in 39 ms for the default 32×32 table -- three orders of magnitude under the fit. The magnitude is what makes an area light energetically right, and it is checked against a brute-force hemisphere integration that shares no code with it, agreeing to two decimals. **The exclusion in that comparison is the honest part**: below roughness 0.2 a uniform grid cannot resolve a near-mirror lobe and the *reference* is the wrong one -- 20% out at roughness 0.05 -- which is exactly why importance sampling exists; and even at roughness 0.2 with a grazing view the reference needs 384 steps to converge, so it is given them rather than the comparison being loosened. One real bug is recorded: the average direction was first measured against the view`s own tangent, but a reflected direction leans *away* from the view, so that component is negative -- and a value that has to fit in [0, 1] to survive an 8-bit texture stored it as zero, flattening the lobe`s tilt away entirely and leaving every table entry pointing at the normal. It is measured against the reflection-side tangent now, and the smooth end of the table reproduces the mirror direction to two decimals. |
| MOD-2062 | Area-light shading, in the engine layer's own effect | ✅ | Delivered as `AreaLightShading` plus a slot on `ClusteredForwardEffect`, not in `PbrEffect`, for the same structural reason as `MOD-2045`: `PbrEffect` owns no shader source, so a light loop there is a change to EasyGL's built-in effect family. **The diffuse half is exact, not approximate** -- a Lambertian surface reflects a clamped cosine, and the irradiance a polygon delivers to one is a closed-form sum over its edges, which is the identity linearly transformed cosines are built on and needs none of the fitted matrix `MOD-2061` refuses to generate. The specular half is a cosine lobe aimed along the BRDF's average reflection direction and widened with roughness, with its **energy** taken from the table: the shape is approximate and the energy is not, which is the half a viewer notices. **All three shapes become one quad**, because the edge sum is what the maths is written for -- a disc is an area-matched rectangle (axes scaled by `sqrt(pi)/2`, so the two enclose the same area), and a tube is a quad turned to face what it lights. The horizon clip is a loop in both the C++ and the GLSL rather than the reference implementation's sixteen-case switch: one place to be wrong instead of sixteen, and the two paths cannot disagree about a case one of them forgot. Exactness is checked against numbers written down beforehand -- a sky-filling light delivers a form factor of exactly 1, an edge-on light exactly 0, a small distant one the inverse square to within 2%, a disc exactly pi/4 of the rectangle around it. **Three bugs are recorded because each produced a plausible frame.** The tube took the component of the direction-to-surface *perpendicular to the axis* as its billboard normal -- the obvious thing to write -- which puts that direction *in* the quad's plane, so a tube directly overhead was seen edge-on and delivered nothing. The shader normalised `normal * nDotV - viewDirection`, which is the zero vector at exactly normal incidence, so the NaN reached the lobe axis and a mirror looked at head-on came back black; the CPU path always had the fallback and the shader did not. And the BRDF table was sampled at N.V = 1, which lands exactly on the texture's seam -- a texture bound through `SetTexture` keeps the default wrap mode and per-unit sampler state does not reach it -- so the filter averaged the last column with the *first*, the grazing end, and told a head-on mirror its reflection leans thirty degrees off the normal. The lookup is remapped onto the first and last texel centres now, and the shader reproduces the CPU model to within one 8-bit step at every roughness from 0.05 to 0.9. |
| MOD-2063 | Tests and documentation, including the absence of area-light shadows | ✅ | Closes 20.7. `docs/cnaext-engine-layer.md` gains a section covering the exact diffuse term, the approximate-in-shape and exact-in-energy specular one, the three-shapes-one-quad reduction, and the one-light-per-draw budget with the reason. **The absence of area-light shadows is stated twice on purpose** -- in the documentation and in `AreaLightEXT`s own header -- because a soft shadow needs many samples of the light`s surface or a ray query and this layer has neither, so an area light lights whatever faces it whether or not something stands in the way; met without warning that looks like a bug in the shadow system rather than a boundary. The refusal of a zero-area light is written down beside it, including why a *tube* is exempt: it is a line with a radius rather than a surface, so parallel axes are meaningful there. Tests total 28 across the three files -- the struct`s validation, the table against a brute-force integration, the form factor against numbers written down beforehand, and the shader against the CPU model. Full suite after the section: 8153 run, 8089 passed, the rest skipped, `CNAEXT_Showcase` still the one red gate (`MOD-2035`). |

### 20.8 Material extensions beyond glTF core (`MOD-2070`–`MOD-2079`)

`PbrMaterial` describes seven texture slots today: base colour, normal, metallic-roughness,
emissive, occlusion and the two `KHR_materials_specular` maps.

| ID | Task | Status | Acceptance criterion |
|---|---|---|---|
| MOD-2070 | `KHR_materials_clearcoat`: factor, roughness, normal map | 🟨 | **Carried in a new `PbrMaterialExtensions` beside a `PbrMaterial` rather than inside one, and the placement is the decision worth the row.** `PbrMaterial`s defining property is that it is *lossless* against `PbrEffect` -- `applyMaterial` then `extractMaterial` returns an equal material, which is what the whole of Phase 13 existed to establish. `PbrEffect` has no clearcoat state and cannot gain shading without changing EasyGL`s generated program (the wall `MOD-2045` and `MOD-2062` already hit), so a clearcoat field on `PbrMaterial` would be silently dropped by that round trip and two materials would compare unequal for a reason nothing in the type explains. The extension set has its own equality, hash and `ToString`, so `MOD-2075`s requirement lands on it rather than being lost. **The shading is a second specular lobe with its own roughness, and it takes from the base exactly what it reflects** -- the test asserts both halves, because a coat that only ever *added* would pass the obvious one and still be wrong: what makes lacquer look like lacquer is that it is brighter where it catches the light and darker everywhere else. A zero factor is bit-for-bit the uncoated result, not merely close. The bound: **the three clearcoat *maps* are carried and not consumed.** `ClusteredForwardEffect` binds no material textures at all -- it has a base colour, a metallic and a roughness rather than a texture set -- so the strength, roughness and normal maps exist for the importer and the round trip and the factors are what shade. One test had to be loosened for a real reason worth recording: a clearcoat roughness of 0.05 gives a lobe about a seventh of a degree wide, and the pixel the GPU comparison reads sits four degrees off the specular direction, so the CPU reference was reading a peak the frame could not contain; the comparison uses 0.35 and the sharp coat is checked on the CPU alone. |
| MOD-2071 | `KHR_materials_sheen`: colour and roughness | 🟨 | Sheen is what makes velvet look like velvet, and the reason it cannot be faked with roughness is that **its distribution is a different shape**: the Charlie lobe peaks where the half-vector is *perpendicular* to the normal, the opposite of a specular lobe, which is why it appears as a rim at grazing angles where the highlight is fading. The test says exactly that -- not "sheen contributes something", but that **its share of the result grows towards grazing**, more than doubling, which an ordinary specular lobe cannot do. The alpha floor of 0.07 is not cosmetic: the exponent is `1 / alpha`, so glTF`s default roughness of 0 gives a rim too tight to survive any sensible resolution. A third test pins that the lobe keeps its own colour rather than being tinted by the base -- a blue rim on a red cushion is a thing velvet does -- and a black sheen is bit-for-bit the unsheened result. A `contribution` overload taking the whole extension set was added alongside the scalar one, because a caller holding a material should not be able to get the order of six trailing floats wrong, and because it picks up a lobe added later without a signature change. **The bound is energy**: glTF scales the base layer down by the sheen`s directional albedo, read from a table, and that table is not generated here -- so a sheened surface is slightly brighter than energy conservation allows, by the sheen`s own (small) albedo. Stated rather than hidden; the alternative was a second generated table for a term that is a few per cent of a dim lobe. |
| MOD-2072 | `KHR_materials_transmission` + `_volume` | ✅ | **Transmission is not transparency, and the difference is what forces the frame copy.** An alpha-blended surface lets the background *through*; a transmissive one **refracts** it, so what is behind a glass sphere is displaced -- the shader bends the view ray at the surface, walks it the volume`s thickness, projects the exit point back to screen space and reads the copy there. **The material is refused rather than approximated when no copy is set**, and the refusal is the row`s point: without one the surface comes back opaque, which is not a slightly wrong glass but the absence of one, and it would read as the extension never having been implemented. Withdrawing the copy refuses it again rather than remembering the old one. The cost is stated where a caller will meet it -- the opaque geometry has to be drawn, resolved and copied before any transmissive surface can be drawn at all, and the layer does not make that copy because only the application knows when its opaque pass ended. The volume absorbs by **Beer`s law**, and the test asserts the number that defines the attenuation distance: after travelling exactly that far, white light *is* the attenuation colour, and twice as far squares it. **One real change to the shading came out of this**: `cnaShade` now returns its diffuse term separately, because glTF replaces the *diffuse* with what is behind the surface and leaves the highlights alone -- blending the whole result would have produced glass with no highlight, which is the thing that stops looking like glass. `setIor` refuses values below 1: a vacuum is the floor of what a material can be, not a value to interpolate through. |
| MOD-2073 | `KHR_materials_ior` and `_iridescence` | ✅ | IOR is effect state (`setIor`, refusing anything below a vacuum, since 1 is the floor of what a material can be rather than a value to interpolate through) and was already needed by `MOD-2072`s refraction. **Iridescence is the interesting half.** A thin film is the one shading term whose correctness is easy to state and easy to get subtly wrong: light reflecting off the *top* of the film and light reflecting off the layer beneath travel different distances, so they arrive out of phase, and which wavelengths cancel depends on thickness *and* angle -- which is why no colour map can stand in for it. `ThinFilmIridescence` implements Belcour and Barla`s model in **both C++ and GLSL, written to mirror each other line for line**, and the two are compared on the GPU across fifteen combinations of thickness and view angle. The tests assert what interference actually does rather than that something appeared: the colour moves with thickness *and its hue changes* (red overtakes blue at least once across a 400 nm sweep, which brightness alone cannot do), the colour moves with angle, the reflectance never leaves [0, 1] over 125 sampled configurations, and a gold base stays recognisably gold under the film because a film modulates what is beneath it rather than replacing it. **One deliberate departure from the reference implementation is recorded**: there the compound reflectance clamps `R12 * R23` to a floor of 1e-5 rather than to zero, so at zero thickness the first interference order survives as a residue of about 0.007 -- small, but *coloured*, meaning a material carrying the extension with the film switched off is not the material without it. A film of no thickness now returns the base`s own Schlick reflectance exactly, so "off" means off. The GPU comparison also refuses to compare against a clamped value: the scale that makes a 0.04-to-0.15 term readable in eight bits clips at grazing, so the sample range stops before that and an assertion says so rather than the test quietly measuring the clamp. |
| MOD-2074 | Subsurface scattering (screen-space burley or a wrapped-diffuse approximation) | 🟨 | **Wrapped diffuse, chosen with the reason, and the header says it is an approximation in the sentence a caller reads first.** Screen-space Burley is a *diffusion*: light enters at one pixel and leaves at another, which needs a diffuse-only buffer separated from the specular, a depth-aware separable blur over it, and MRT to produce that buffer -- and MRT is the capability this layer already had to build a two-pass fallback for (`MOD-2040`). What is here is per-pixel: light wraps past the terminator, and a back-scatter term glows when the light is behind the surface. It captures the *look* of skin, wax and a leaf without simulating anything travelling sideways, and the honest cost is stated plainly: **a thin object lit from behind glows, and a thick one glows exactly as much, because nothing here knows how thick it is.** A black subsurface colour is bit-for-bit the ordinary result even with the wrap turned all the way up. **One real bug came out of the tests and is worth the row**: with the light exactly behind the surface, `L` is `-V`, so the half-vector`s sum is the zero vector and normalising it is a NaN -- which then survives being multiplied by a zero `N·L` and paints the pixel black. That configuration is not exotic; it is precisely the one the back-scatter exists for, so the test that exercises it was the test that found it. Both the CPU and the shader guard it now, and the early-out that skips a light behind the surface had to learn about the back-scatter too. |
| MOD-2075 | Extend the value semantics and the round-trip tests to every new field | ✅ | The row assumed the new fields would live on `PbrMaterial`; `MOD-2070` established they cannot, so the requirement lands on `PbrMaterialExtensions` instead and the two halves are both covered. On the extension set: **a table-driven completeness test that mutates each of the twenty-four fields in turn and requires both `operator==` and `GetHashCode` to notice** -- the same shape as the test that has caught every field added to `PbrMaterial` since `MOD-1300`, and the reason it is worth having is that a field added later and forgotten in one of the two fails here rather than in a game six months on. Copy and assignment are checked to carry every field, including through `ToString`. On `PbrMaterial`: the boundary itself is now asserted rather than merely intended -- its round trip through `PbrEffect` stays exact, *and it stays exact because there is nothing extra in it*, which is the sentence the new test carries. |
| MOD-2076 | glTF importer support for all of the above | ✅ | **cgltf had parsed all five extensions for years; what was missing was anyone copying the values out**, so an application could not see them at all -- which is the quietest kind of gap, because the material imports, looks ordinary, and nothing says the file asked for anything else. `MaterialOut` now carries clearcoat, sheen, transmission, volume and iridescence as plain values, each defaulted to the *extension`s own* default, so a file declaring none of them produces byte-for-byte the material it did before. The engine-layer side is `materialExtensionsFromGltfEXT`, expressed against **a second concept rather than a widened first one** -- folding the new fields into `GltfMaterialSourceEXT` would have turned every existing call site into a compile error, and the whole reason the bridge is a concept is that graphics-ext must not link the content module. The registry in `GltfImportCore` is deliberately **not** touched: it describes what the default runtime path does, and that path still does not shade these lobes, so claiming them "Implemented" there would be a false statement about `PbrEffect`. **One bug found by a test**: glTF spells "the medium absorbs nothing" as `+Infinity`, but cgltf`s sentinel for the absent value is `FLT_MAX` -- so an `std::isfinite` guard let 3.4e38 through as a real attenuation distance, and every volume with the extension`s default would have absorbed everything after one unit. It is a magnitude test now. Two repository gates had to be answered rather than worked around: the new suite was added to conformance rung **L3** beside the other material suites, and the inline-document ratchet was raised 263 → 268 with the decision it asks for recorded in place -- these are **loader-machinery probes**, not conformance statements about an asset, so `tools/gltf_fixtures/` is the wrong home for them. |
| MOD-2077 | Documentation and the shader-cost note | ✅ | Closes 20.8. `docs/cnaext-engine-layer.md` gains a section covering why the extensions are a separate type from `PbrMaterial`, what each lobe is and is not, and the one bound they share -- **the maps are carried, not consumed**, because `ClusteredForwardEffect` binds no material textures at all. **The cost note is measured rather than asserted, and what it found is a negative result worth stating**: at 64 lights per fragment, three runs put every configuration -- each lobe alone, and all four at once -- between 21.0 ms and 24.0 ms with **no consistent ordering**, several reading *faster* than the baseline in a given run. The lobes` cost is below this machine`s noise, because with 69 lights in the busiest cluster the light loop dominates so completely that a per-fragment lobe does not register. The note says that plainly, and says what it is *not*: not evidence the lobes are free on hardware, since each is compiled into the shader whether or not its factor is zero and a real GPU charges for the register pressure through occupancy, which a software rasteriser cannot see. The structural half of the note is separate from the measurement: each lobe sits behind a dynamic branch on its own factor, so a fragment whose lobe is off pays a branch rather than the lobe. Full suite after the section: 8194 run, 8130 passed, the rest skipped, `CNAEXT_Showcase` still the one red gate (`MOD-2035`). |

### 20.9 Global illumination (`MOD-2080`–`MOD-2089`)

Today the only indirect light is IBL from one static environment map, applied uniformly.

| ID | Task | Status | Acceptance criterion |
|---|---|---|---|
| MOD-2080 | `LightProbeEXT`: an irradiance probe with a position, built by `EnvironmentProcessor` | ✅ | `ImageBasedLightEXT` lights a whole scene from one environment applied uniformly -- every surface gets the same ambient whether it stands in the doorway or at the back of the cellar. A probe is the answer to *where*. **Stored as second-order spherical harmonics, and that is what makes a grid affordable**: an irradiance cube per probe would be kilobytes and could not be blended with its neighbours, while nine coefficients can be averaged directly, because the projection is linear -- the average of two probes` coefficients *is* the projection of the average of their light. A test asserts exactly that, since it is the property `MOD-2081` depends on and nothing else would catch. Built through `EnvironmentProcessor::generateProbe`, reusing the same cube-reading path the irradiance convolution uses but a *cheaper* integral: projection visits each texel of the environment once, where convolution visits each texel of the output and integrates a hemisphere for it. **The texel`s solid angle is computed rather than assumed** -- a cube`s texels do not subtend equal angles, and weighting them equally tilts every probe towards the eight corners of its own cube, a bias with no physical cause. The evaluation is Ramamoorthi and Hanrahan`s, and the tests pin it against numbers written down beforehand: a uniform environment of radiance L delivers exactly π·L to *every* normal, which fixes every scale factor at once; and with five faces black and one white, the normal facing the lit face receives the most and the opposite one the least -- checked on all six faces, because a sign error on a linear basis function swaps exactly those two and changes nothing else. Irradiance is clamped at zero: a least-squares fit to a dark environment overshoots below it, and negative irradiance is light being *removed* from a surface. **One test was wrong and the code was right**: the hand-written constant-term expectation multiplied by `Y00` twice, while the end-to-end projection beside it already agreed with π·L. |
| MOD-2081 | `LightProbeVolumeEXT`: a grid of probes with trilinear interpolation | ✅ | A probe answers "what does the light look like *here*"; a volume answers it everywhere inside a box. Blending is an ordinary trilinear interpolation **of the coefficients themselves**, which is only meaningful because `MOD-2080` established that the projection is linear -- so the blend of eight probes` coefficients is the projection of the blend of their light, and the result is a valid probe rather than an approximation of one. The acceptance criterion is met and sharpened: moving across the volume the ambient rises monotonically at every step, and **halfway is exactly halfway** rather than merely somewhere between. Two tests exist because a wrong grid still looks right: **an off-by-one in the index arithmetic produces a perfectly smooth gradient between the wrong pair of probes**, so sampling at each probe`s own position must return that probe unchanged, and each axis is blended alone to catch a volume that used one axis` weight for another -- which would also interpolate, just along the wrong direction. **Outside the volume clamps rather than going dark**: a character stepping one unit past the last probe should not lose its ambient entirely, and clamping is wrong slowly where falling back to nothing is wrong suddenly. The blended probe also reports a position inside the box, so a caller cannot be told the light came from somewhere the volume does not reach. `setProbe` overwrites the probe`s own position with the grid`s, because a probe placed elsewhere makes the weights describe one arrangement and the light another -- which reads as the lighting lagging behind the geometry. A single-probe axis is legal and answers that one probe everywhere, rather than dividing by a zero span. The volume knows nothing about walls, and that is stated where a reader meets it: `MOD-2083` is where it gets its answer. |
| MOD-2082 | Probe selection and blending, in the engine layer`s own effect | 🟨 | Delivered on `ClusteredForwardEffect` rather than `PbrEffect`, for the third time and the same structural reason (`MOD-2045`, `MOD-2062`, `MOD-2070`). A probe or a volume replaces the effect`s flat ambient term; with neither, the flat term is exactly what it was. **The bound is that the ambient is per-draw, not per-pixel, and it is a deliberate trade rather than an omission**: evaluating a volume per fragment means fetching eight probes` twenty-seven coefficients each -- over two hundred texture reads for the term that is, by construction, the smoothest thing in the frame. One probe per object is what that smoothness buys; what it costs is that a large object crossing a lighting boundary does not show the gradient across itself, and the header says so and says what to do about it. The volume is sampled at the world matrix`s translation, so two draws of the same geometry at two places are lit differently -- which is the whole point of a grid and the one thing a single global environment cannot do. **The conversion is checked as a number, not a trend**: a probe of radiance L lights a Lambertian surface to `albedo × L`, because the probe carries irradiance `π·L` and the surface reflects `albedo/π` of it -- and the frame agrees to within one 8-bit step at both ends of the volume and at its midpoint. A probe and a volume are mutually exclusive, since two sources of one term would leave the answer depending on the order they were set in. **One test was measuring the wrong thing and is recorded**: it moved the object ten units to sample the volume`s ends, which slid the wall partly out of frame, so the centre pixel it read was not on the wall at all -- the frame`s brightest pixel was the expected value the whole time. The volume is one unit wide now, and the test asserts the wall covers every pixel before reading any of them. |
| MOD-2083 | Probe-visibility leak reduction | ✅ | The defect this fixes is the one that makes a naive probe grid unusable indoors: a probe in a lit room and a surface in the dark room next door are *neighbours in the grid*, and nothing about a grid knows there is a wall between them. A probe now records **how far the geometry is along each of six axis directions, as two moments**, and a corner`s trilinear weight is multiplied by a Chebyshev test against that -- exactly a variance shadow map, applied to a probe. Both moments are stored because a hard edge and a soft one must behave differently: a flat wall has almost no variance and cuts off sharply, a cluttered direction has a lot and fades, and a test asserts both. **Three decisions are pinned rather than left to the code`s shape.** Rejecting a corner *renormalises* rather than darkens -- a surface beside a wall should be lit by the probes that can see it, not by a fraction of the ones that cannot, and with two equally bright probes the result must stay at their common value. A point rejected by *every* corner falls back to the plain blend, so it **leaks rather than going black**: a leak is wrong, a hole in the lighting is more visible, and the choice is asserted instead of being an accident. And a probe with nothing recorded is trusted completely, because every probe in every volume built before this row has nothing recorded and the fix must not darken them. The direction blends across the up-to-three axes it points along rather than snapping to the nearest: snapping makes the weight jump as a surface turns, and a discontinuity in an ambient term is more visible than the leak it was fixing. The leak test is paired with its own control -- the same scene with the wall removed leaks, which is what makes the first half about the visibility test rather than about the geometry. |
| MOD-2084 | Baking: an offline path that renders probes from the scene | ✅ | `LightProbeBaker` hands out a view and a 90-degree projection six times per probe; the application draws whatever it wants seen, and the layer turns the six results into nine coefficients. Same division as shadow maps and the prepass, for the same reason -- the layer has no idea what a scene *is*. **The directions are reconstructed from the view matrices themselves rather than from a cube-map face layout**, which removes the entire class of bug a cube capture usually ships with: there is no convention to agree with, only the matrix that was used. The test that pins it lights one face at a time and asks the baked probe which way the light came from, **on all six** -- a mirrored or rotated face passes every smoothness check and fails exactly this. The energy half reaches the same number `MOD-2080` was pinned against, now through six *rendered* faces instead of a cube: white in every direction delivers exactly π to every normal. Visibility (`MOD-2083`) is a **second, separate pass**, because it needs the scene drawn as distance from the probe -- a different shader from the one that draws it lit, and only the application has either; the layer reduces the captures to the two moments the leak test wants. Each pass keeps what the other left behind, and a test says so in both directions. **A bug in the support probe is recorded**: it bound the *capture* target and read one texel back, but `GetData` wants the whole surface -- so every renderer looked incapable and all six real tests skipped. It probes a one-pixel target now. Two more decisions are stated where they are made: the visibility capture clears to **white**, because an unwritten pixel has to read as the far plane -- clearing to black would record a wall at the camera in every uncovered direction and reject every probe in the volume; and the face size is small on purpose, since irradiance is the lowest-frequency signal in the frame and a bigger capture costs six times its area per probe for almost nothing. |
| MOD-2085 | Lightmaps: decide, with the reason | ⛔ | **Refused, and the reason is that the missing piece is a tool rather than a renderer.** A lightmap needs three things CNA does not have and would not gain by adding runtime code: a **UV unwrapper**, which is a mesh-processing problem with its own decade of literature; an **atlas packer** to place the charts; and a bake that rasterises *into UV space* to recover a world position and normal per texel. The second UV channel itself is not the obstacle -- `PbrMaterial` already carries two, and `textureCoordinateSetsEXT` already routes them -- which is exactly what makes the refusal specific: the data path exists and the *authoring* does not. Nor would importing solve it: glTF assets in the wild almost never ship a lightmap UV, so a runtime that read one would find nothing to read. The honest place for this is an offline tool beside `gltf_to_cnj`, and until such a tool exists a lightmap runtime would be a reader with no writer. **What the layer offers instead is the probe volume this section built** (`MOD-2080`–`MOD-2084`): coarser, and unlike a lightmap it lights *moving* objects, which is the half a lightmap never could. |
| MOD-2086 | Voxel-cone-traced GI: decide, with the reason | ⛔ | **Refused, and this time the arithmetic is the argument.** Voxelisation writes a 3D texture from a fragment shader, which needs image stores -- and `MOD-1514` established that GL ES refuses image bindings for CNA`s textures because it wants immutable storage and CNA allocates mutably. `MOD-2052` already met the same wall for froxel fog and worked around it with a **slice atlas** in a 2D target, so the obvious question is whether GI could do the same. It cannot, and the numbers say why: fog`s froxel volume is a few tens of slices at screen-tile resolution, while a usable voxel GI volume is 128³ -- two million voxels, which as a slice atlas is a **128 by 16384** target, past the 8192 limit a great many GL ES implementations publish and well past anything a mip chain could be built over. Cone tracing then needs *mipmapped* 3D sampling, and a slice atlas has no third dimension to filter across at all: `ColorGradePass` interpolates its blue axis by hand between two lookups, which is affordable once per fragment and not affordable per cone step. So voxel GI is reachable on desktop GL only, and taking it would make this the layer`s **first desktop-only subsystem** -- against a plan whose whole shape is EasyGL first and GL ES 3.0 as the floor. The probe volume is what stands in its place, and the difference is stated rather than glossed: probes carry *irradiance* and cannot produce a glossy bounce or a sharp indirect shadow, which is precisely what cone tracing is for. |
