| MOD-2087 | Tests, perf and documentation | ✅ | Closes 20.9. `docs/cnaext-engine-layer.md` gains a section on the probe path -- why nine coefficients, why irradiance rather than radiance, how the leak test works, why the ambient is per-draw, and the two refusals with their reasons; `docs/cnaext-perf.md` gains the numbers. **The density question is answered as a number**: a probe is **168 bytes** -- nine coefficients, six visibility directions with two moments each, and a position -- so an 8×4×8 grid of 256 probes is **42 KB**, and a test asserts the struct has not quietly grown past what those account for. Capture and projection cost **3.512 ms per probe** at 32×32 per face, and that figure is deliberately measured with a draw that renders **nothing**: what it reports is the layer`s own six binds, six read-backs and one projection, not somebody`s scene. The sentence beside it is the one that matters -- a real bake adds six scene draws per probe, so the same 8×4×8 grid is 1536 scene draws before the layer does anything at all, which is what makes baking an offline operation rather than a slow frame. Section total: 34 tests across the probe, the volume and the baker. Full suite after the section: 8230 run, 8166 passed, the rest skipped, `CNAEXT_Showcase` still the one red gate (`MOD-2035`). |

### 20.10 GPU-driven rendering and display output (`MOD-2090`–`MOD-2099`)

| ID | Task | Status | Acceptance criterion |
|---|---|---|---|
| MOD-2090 | Indirect draw: `DrawIndirect`/`DrawIndexedIndirect` on `IGraphicsRenderer` + capability | ✅ | `GraphicsCapability::IndirectDraw`, derived from `IGraphicsRenderer::SupportsIndirectDrawEXT()` for the reason `ComputeShaders` is — a renderer whose capability switch ends in `default: return true` must not claim an entry point it has never called. The argument layouts are written down once as `CNA::IndirectDrawArguments` / `CNA::IndirectDrawIndexedArguments` in `modules/graphics/include/CNA/`, with `static_assert`s on their size, because the GPU reads those bytes verbatim: the layout is the contract between a compute shader and a draw call, not an implementation detail. `GraphicsDevice::DrawPrimitivesIndirectEXT` / `DrawIndexedPrimitivesIndirectEXT` build the same `GpuDrawParams` the ordinary routes build, fold the binding offset the same way, and deliberately leave `vertexStart`/`startIndex`/`baseVertex` at the folded value alone — the command's own `FirstVertex`/`FirstIndex`/`BaseVertex` are those, and setting both would apply each offset twice. **The honest half of this row is what it cannot check.** Every other draw route validates that the requested range fits the bound buffers; that check cannot exist here, because the range is in GPU memory when the command is issued. A wrong count is undefined behaviour rather than an exception, and the shader that wrote it owns that. What the CPU still checks — a bound vertex buffer, a bound index buffer, an applied effect, and arguments that are 4-byte aligned and lie inside their buffer — it checks exactly as before. EasyGL implements both through one private `IssueIndirectDrawEXT`, modelled on the *instanced* route rather than the simple one, because an indirect command always carries an instance count whether or not anything sets it above 1 — so per-instance streams, their divisors and their teardown all work, which is what `MOD-2091` will need. The version floor is asked as its own question: ES 3.1, but desktop GL **4.0**, not compute's 4.3, since indirect draws arrived three versions earlier and a context between the two really can draw indirectly without dispatching. `GraphicsMemoryBarrier::IndirectCommand` is a new bit (GL's `GL_COMMAND_BARRIER_BIT`) and is deliberately distinct from `ShaderStorage`: writing a count through a storage binding and *fetching* it as a command are two accesses, and ordering only the first lets the command fetch read the previous frame's numbers. 9 cases. The test that carries the row draws the same triangle, the same effect and the same target three times and changes **one word of GPU memory** between them — one instance, zero instances, one vertex — so a route that quietly used a CPU count would produce the same picture all three times and fail. A control draw through the ordinary route runs first, because an assertion about an indirect draw means nothing if the geometry never reached the target at all. **Known wrinkle, not worked around:** CNA's only argument buffer is `CNA::Graphics::StorageBuffer`, which is an SSBO and therefore needs ES 3.1 / GL 4.3. On a desktop context between 4.0 and 4.2 the capability truthfully reports `true` and there is still no CNA type able to hold the arguments. Reporting a lower floor to hide that would be the wrong repair; the tests skip on `IndirectDraw && ComputeShaders` and say so. |
| MOD-2091 | GPU culling that feeds indirect arguments rather than reading back | ✅ | `GpuInstanceCuller`. `MOD-1551` proved a compute shader culls the *same* objects `FrustumCullerEXT` does; what it left was that the verdict came back to the CPU to be turned into draw calls, which is a stall rather than a copy. The shader now compacts the survivors and `atomicAdd`s into **`cnaCommand[1]`, which is the indirect command's `InstanceCount` word itself** — the same atomic that reserves an instance's slot is what tells the draw how much to draw, so there is no second pass and no count to carry anywhere. The consequence that shapes the API: a compute shader cannot write a vertex buffer in this profile, so the per-instance world matrix reaches the draw through a storage buffer the **vertex shader** reads by `gl_InstanceID`. That needs `GL_MAX_VERTEX_SHADER_STORAGE_BLOCKS >= 1`, which ES 3.1 permits to be **zero** — a context can implement compute in full and still refuse an SSBO in a vertex stage — so it is a fourth requirement, probed through a new `IGraphicsRenderer::GetMaxVertexShaderStorageBlocksEXT()`, alongside a new `BindStorageBufferForDrawEXT()` (the binding points are context state shared by every stage, so the call that feeds a dispatch feeds a draw). **It refuses rather than falls back**, unlike `ClusteredLightCompute`, and the difference is not a matter of taste: that class's CPU path is a correct if slower answer, while there is no CPU equivalent of "the draw call came from the GPU" — a silent fallback would report success for a frame that never removed the stall. `getUnsupportedReason()` names which of the four requirements is missing. The instance matrices go across column-major, the same form a custom effect's own `World` uniform arrives in, so `cnaInstanceWorld()` multiplies exactly like `World` and a shader can swap one for the other. `readVisibleCountEXT()` exists and is documented as **the stall this class exists to avoid**, named so nobody puts it in a frame by accident; the rendering path never calls it. 8 cases. The count is compared against `FrustumCullerEXT` on a scene deliberately split 5 visible / 6 not, and then the frame is inspected: five instances laid out as separated vertical bands, counted as runs across the middle row, so *fewer* means a survivor was lost and *more* means one was drawn twice or a culled one slipped through. A second cull with the camera turned away must report zero, which is what catches a command whose count was not reset. |
| MOD-2092 | HDR display output: `SurfaceFormat` and swap-chain colour space | ✅ | `CNA::DisplayColorSpace` (`Srgb`, `Scrgb`, `Hdr10`) on the renderer boundary as `GetDisplayColorSpaceEXT()` / `SetDisplayColorSpaceEXT()`, and `CNA::Graphics::HdrDisplayOutput` for the encoding. **The honest half first: no CNA platform back end offers an HDR swap chain, so every renderer answers `Srgb` and refuses anything else.** That refusal is the deliverable, not a gap left open — an HDR swap chain is a property of the *presentation* path (DXGI, a Vulkan surface format, a platform's own opt-in), and a renderer that accepted the request without reconfiguring one would have every caller encode for a display that is not there. PQ pixels shown as sRGB are washed out and grey, which is a worse frame than SDR that is simply correct. `SupportsDisplayColorSpaceEXT` is answered by trying and putting back rather than by a second query, so the two answers cannot drift apart, and a test asserts a refused request left the state alone. The encoding itself is complete and testable without such a display, because it is arithmetic: ST 2084 (PQ) with the standard's exact ratios rather than rounded decimals, the Rec. 709→2020 matrix, a Reinhard roll-off against the peak instead of a clip, and scRGB's `paperWhite/80` scale. Written twice — GLSL and C++ — and compared against each other on the GPU in both HDR spaces, with the comparison guarded by *"did the pass change anything at all"*, which is what caught the first version of the test: scRGB at 80-nit paper white is the identity, so the agreement would have held for a pass that did nothing. Paper white for that space is 40 nits in the test instead. **In `Srgb` the pass copies through pixel for pixel**, asserted with `EXPECT_EQ` per channel, so it can be left in the chain on every machine — the row's "SDR output unchanged" as a test rather than a claim. The three enum ordinals are pinned by a test too, since the shader branches on the plain integer. 11 cases; EasyGL. |
| MOD-2093 | FSR 1 spatial upscaling as a pass | ✅ | `SpatialUpscalePass`, written from the published description of FSR 1's shape rather than from a vendor SDK, and not bit-identical to AMD's reference: an edge-adaptive upsample followed by a contrast-adaptive sharpen that is **clamped to the four-neighbour min/max**, which is the part that stops a sharpener ringing at a hard edge. The row's own acceptance criterion is a test rather than a claim — `AOneToOneScaleCopiesThroughPixelForPixel` compares every channel of every texel with `EXPECT_EQ`, not a tolerance, and it runs with sharpening left at its default, because the identity has to hold for a pass a game left configured and not only for one that was turned off first. A 1:1 draw therefore also forces sharpness to zero: the pass is asked to change nothing, and a sharpen is a change. `setEdgeAdaptive(false)` exists so the adaptive path can be measured against the plain bilinear stretch it claims to beat: the comparison renders the same diagonal through both, finds each row's mid-tone crossing to sub-pixel precision, and asserts the RMS deviation from the best-fit line is *lower* — a staircase is exactly a set of crossings that do not lie on a line, so this measures the claim instead of eyeballing it, and it fails if the two paths are secretly the same code. 8 cases; EasyGL. |
| MOD-2094 | Decals: screen-space projected decals using the prepass depth | ✅ | `DecalPass`. For every pixel the prepass depth gives a position, the decal's inverse transform puts it in the decal's own space, and it is painted only if it lands inside the decal's unit box. No geometry is generated and the receiving mesh knows nothing about it. **The box is the whole contract**, and the row's own acceptance criterion is the test: two quads at very different depths, one filling the left half of the view and one the right, and a decal box placed around the near one only. The left half is painted and the right is untouched — a pass that forgot the box's *depth* extent would paint both and would look entirely convincing in any screenshot with one surface in it. A second case moves the same box between the two surfaces and asserts nothing at all is painted, which is what a pass that quietly painted whatever was in front of it would fail. With prepass normals supplied, a slope test drops pixels the decal only grazes — the other classic decal artefact, a texture smeared across a surface nearly parallel to the projection axis. It is tested by turning the *same* box a quarter turn: every pixel is still inside it, and none may be painted. Two deliberate departures from the rest of the layer, each for one reason: this is the only pass that composites rather than replaces, so it owns its own `SpriteBatch` at `BlendState::NonPremultiplied` instead of using `FullscreenPass`'s `Opaque`; and `isInsideDecalBox` is offered as a static so the rule can be checked without a GPU and a game can ask before spending a fullscreen pass. A pixel with no surface at all (depth at the far plane) is discarded rather than painted, which is how a decal ends up floating in the sky. 8 cases; EasyGL. |
| MOD-2095 | A particle system as a subsystem rather than a demo | ✅ | `ParticleSystem`: an emitter (`ParticleEmitterSettings`), a simulation that runs on the GPU where the device has compute and on the CPU where it does not, and one instanced draw of camera-facing billboards. The simulation is written twice, and the point of the row is that the two are **one simulation and not two**: the spawn values come from an integer hash written identically in GLSL and C++, and integer arithmetic wraps the same in both, so every value a spawn draws is bit-identical rather than merely similar. The GLSL deliberately writes `1.0 + (cos(a) - 1.0) * u` instead of `mix(1.0, cos(a), u)` — `mix` is `x*(1-a) + y*a`, a different float expression — so the only thing left that can differ is rounding in the integration. The comparison test steps both for 40 frames and asserts the **generation counter matches exactly** before comparing positions within 1e-3: an integer difference there would mean the two disagreed about *when* a particle died, which is a different bug from disagreeing about where it was, and it asserts that at least one particle actually respawned so the respawn half is not skipped. **Nothing is read back to draw.** The vertex shader reads the same buffer the compute shader wrote, indexed by `gl_InstanceID`, so the whole system is one instanced draw — which brings the same vertex-stage-SSBO requirement `MOD-2091` found, plus `Instancing`. Unlike `GpuInstanceCuller`, falling back here is right: the CPU path produces the same particles more slowly, so a device without compute gets a correct effect rather than an empty screen, and its draw goes through the stock textured vertex-coloured effect with billboards built on the CPU — no shader of its own, because the whole point of that path is running where custom shaders do not. `setSimulationOnCpuEXT` pins the simulation to the CPU on a device that has both, for two honest reasons: a tile GPU where a dispatch plus its barrier costs more than stepping a few hundred particles, and **a fallback nothing exercises is a fallback nobody knows is broken** — without it neither the CPU step loop nor the CPU draw path would ever run in this repository's suite. Switching carries the particles across rather than restarting them. Two decisions recorded in place: emission rate times lifetime decides how many slots are live, and a rate the capacity cannot sustain is **clamped with `isEmissionRateClamped()` saying so** rather than quietly emitting fewer than the settings claim; and `reset()` staggers ages across one lifetime, because a system whose particles all start at zero emits one puff and then nothing for a lifetime. A step that overshoots a lifetime carries the remainder into the new particle's age, so a long frame does not silently shorten every lifetime it spans. 12 cases; EasyGL. |

### 20.11 Refused against this profile, with reasons (`MOD-2096`–`MOD-2099`)

Recorded as rows so the refusal is visible in the same table as the work, rather than being an
absence a reader has to infer.

| ID | Task | Status | Acceptance criterion |
|---|---|---|---|
| MOD-2096 | Hardware ray tracing (DXR / `VK_KHR_ray_tracing`) | ⛔ | **Not reachable from the reference renderer.** Neither GL ES 3.x nor desktop GL 3.3 has any ray-tracing API; there is nothing for EasyGL to call. This is a renderer-capability question for `plan_vulkan.md` and `plan_dx.md`, and it would need an acceleration-structure abstraction on `IGraphicsRenderer` before this layer could express it. Not a permanent refusal — a refusal against the only renderer this phase targets. |
| MOD-2097 | Mesh shaders / task shaders | ⛔ | **Not reachable from the reference renderer.** Mesh shaders are GL 4.6 + `NV_mesh_shader`/`EXT_mesh_shader`, D3D12 or Vulkan; the EasyGL profile floor is GL ES 3.0. `MOD-2090`'s indirect draw is the part of GPU-driven rendering that *is* reachable here, which is why it is a task and this is not. |
| MOD-2098 | DLSS / XeSS / FSR 2+ temporal upscaling | ⛔ | **Vendor SDKs and a temporal pipeline this layer does not have.** DLSS and XeSS are closed vendor SDKs bound to D3D12/Vulkan; FSR 2 and above are temporal and need motion vectors and a history buffer — the same requirement that put TAA out of scope in `MOD-610`. FSR 1 is spatial and pure shader arithmetic, so it is `MOD-2093` rather than a refusal. |
| MOD-2099 | Virtual texturing / streamed terrain | ⛔ | **An asset-pipeline problem wearing a renderer's clothes.** Sparse/virtual textures need `ARB_sparse_texture`, absent from GL ES entirely, and a software fallback needs a tile store, a feedback pass and a streaming budget — a subsystem larger than this whole phase, and one whose hard part is the offline tool rather than the runtime. Out of scope, and named so nobody plans it by accident. |


### 20.12 The section's own numbers (`MOD-2100`)

> `MOD-2100` is one past the range this phase declared (`MOD-2000`–`MOD-2099`), and it is numbered
> that way on purpose: 20.10's six IDs were spent on its six subsystems and 20.11's four on its four
> refusals, so measuring the section needed an ID the phase did not have. Renumbering to make room
> would invalidate every cross-reference in `NEXT_modern.md`, `docs/cnaext-engine-layer.md` and
> `docs/cnaext-perf.md` — a worse trade than one row past the boundary, said out loud.

| ID | Task | Status | Acceptance criterion |
|---|---|---|---|
| MOD-2100 | Section 20.10's costs, measured | ✅ | `cna_test_cnaext_gpu_driven`, a new `CNAEXT_GpuDriven` gate and the first numbers §20.10 has had. Four checks — the renderer offers the route or SKIPs; **an indirect draw puts the same pixels on screen as the ordinary draw it replaces** (identity, not "it drew something"); GPU culling keeps exactly the instances the CPU culler keeps; the upscale at 1:1 is the frame it was given. `--benchmark` measures every new path **against the one it claims to beat, in the same frame**, and the numbers are in `docs/cnaext-perf.md`. Two of them are worth stating here because the obvious reading is wrong. **GPU culling loses to CPU culling at 256 objects and draws level at 1024** — which is what a software rasteriser must produce, since a dispatch here is CPU work plus driver overhead. `MOD-2091` never claimed the arithmetic was faster; it claimed the answer stops coming back to the CPU, and llvmpipe has no pipeline to stall, so this measurement cannot see the thing being bought. That is recorded in the *Not measured yet* section rather than left as an implication. And **the particle crossover is real**: the CPU step is 2–4× faster at 1024 particles, the GPU 2× faster at 8192. Both halves are the same simulation, so the choice is purely cost — which is exactly what `setSimulationOnCpuEXT` is for, and it stops being a testing affordance and becomes a tuning one. The indirect draw itself is **indistinguishable** from the ordinary one at this frame size: the two overlap across runs, so its overhead is below the noise floor rather than measurably zero, and the row says so. |


---

## Phase 21 — What Phase 20 still did not cover (`MOD-2101`–`MOD-2199`)

> **Created 2026-08-20**, by the same method that produced Phase 20: look at what the layer has,
> ask what a game would reach for and not find, and check each answer against the code before
> writing it down rather than after. Every gap below was verified absent by grep, not assumed —
> and the largest one is stated in the layer's own documentation today
> (`MaterialBinding.hpp`: *"Draw order still belongs to the application: CNA does not sort."*).
>
> **EasyGL only**, on the same owner decision that governed Phase 20 (`OQ-2`, `OQ-7`). No
> per-renderer rollout section: Phase 16 established that no other renderer executes this layer's
> shader source.
>
> **One boundary this phase does not cross.** `OQ-6` decided the scene-draw contract is app-driven:
> the pipeline takes a callback, it does not own a scene. Nothing here becomes a scene graph.
> `TransparentDrawList` is a *sorting aid* an application fills and the layer orders — it decides
> when draws happen, never what they are.

### 21.1 Transparency, which the layer has no story for at all (`MOD-2101`–`MOD-2110`)

The single largest gap. Every subsystem in Phases 0–20 assumes opaque geometry: the prepass writes
one depth per pixel, SSAO and SSR and fog and motion blur all reconstruct from that one depth, and
`applyMaterialState` sets `BlendState::NonPremultiplied` for a `Blend` material and then says out
loud that ordering is the application's problem. A game with a window in it has nothing to use.

| ID | Task | Status | Acceptance criterion |
|---|---|---|---|
| MOD-2101 | `TransparentDrawList`: submit (bounds, callback), sort back-to-front against a camera, invoke | ✅ | A bounding box and a callable per entry; no geometry is owned or copied, and `clear()` forgets everything. **Not a scene graph** (`OQ-6`): it decides *when* draws happen, never what they are. `clear()` is separate from `drawSorted()` on purpose, because a game drawing the same transparent set into several views — a reflection, a shadow, a cube face — submits once and draws several times, and a list that consumed itself would force a resubmit per view. `getSortedOrderEXT` exposes the order without running anything. 9 cases. |
| MOD-2102 | Stable ordering for equal depths, and a documented tie-break | ✅ | `std::stable_sort` on the distance **alone** — no secondary key, so submission order is the tie-break and it is the same on every standard library. Tested with four boxes on a circle around the eye, every one exactly as far away, so the comparator can express no preference at all; drawn twice in one test, because an unstable sort is allowed to be consistent by luck once. The reason matters more than the mechanism: an unstable tie-break makes a frame flicker between orderings that are each individually defensible, and it does it *only* where geometry is aligned on purpose. |
| MOD-2103 | Sorting by *nearest point of the bounds*, not by centre | ✅ | The key is the distance from the eye to the camera position **clamped into the box**, which is zero on every axis the eye is already between — so an eye inside the bounds sorts at 0 rather than at a negative or a NaN. The test builds the case that separates the two rules and asserts the separation *first*: a long box whose centre is 18 units away but whose near end is 6, against a short box at 10. A centre sort draws the long one first and composites it behind something it visibly passes in front of. The eye position is recovered through `Matrix::Invert` rather than by negating and rotating the translation row, because that shortcut is only correct for a rigid view matrix and a game may hand this one a scale. |
| MOD-2104 | `RenderPipeline` runs a transparent phase after the opaque one and before post-process | ✅ | `setTransparentScene` plus `RenderPipelineSettings::setTransparencyMode` (`None` / `Sorted` / `OrderIndependent`). It draws into the **same target the opaque half did**, before anything is unbound, so transparent geometry is tonemapped and graded with the rest — a surface composited after the tonemapper would be the one thing in the frame that was never scene-referred. The opt-in promise is asserted the strong way: the frame with a transparent draw *registered* and the mode left at `None` is pixel-identical to the frame without one. **The order-independent path set itself a trap and the pipeline had to disarm it.** It unbinds the scene target to accumulate into its own and binds it again to resolve, and the default `DiscardContents` throws the whole opaque frame away on that second bind — so the scene target is created `PreserveContents`, but **only** when that mode is selected, because preserving is not free on a tiler and no other phase in this pipeline returns to a target it left. A test reads the half of the frame that has no transparency on it at all, which is exactly the opaque background, and would have caught the discard. Where the mode is requested and unavailable the pipeline **falls back to `Sorted` and names the missing requirement** through `getTransparencyFallbackReasonEXT`, rather than silently drawing a different way. 5 cases. |
| MOD-2105 | The prepass and the transparent phase agree on who writes depth | ✅ | `DepthStencilState::DepthRead` for the phase: testing **on** so opaque geometry in front still occludes, writing **off** so one transparent surface does not hide the ones behind it. Asserted by drawing the near surface *first* and requiring the far one to survive — with depth writing on it would be rejected, and the symptom is missing geometry rather than anything that looks like a state error, which is why this is its own row rather than a line in `MOD-2104`. |
| MOD-2106 | `WeightedBlendedTransparency`: the accumulation and revealage targets, and the resolve | ✅ | McGuire and Bavoil's weighted blended OIT, **in one geometry pass**, which took a reformulation to reach. The published technique accumulates revealage multiplicatively while colour accumulates additively — a different blend function per draw buffer, which is `glBlendFunci`, GL ES **3.2** or desktop GL 4.0, above this layer's floor and absent from CNA's `BlendState` besides. Revealage is instead accumulated as the **sum of `log(1 - alpha)`**, which is additive, so both targets share one blend state; the resolve exponentiates it back. The alternative was a second pass over every transparent surface. `One`/`One` rather than `BlendState::Additive`, which is `SourceAlpha`/`One` and would scale each contribution by its own alpha a second time on top of the emit's premultiply. The claim is asserted **and its precision is stated**: the same pair in either order differs by at most **1/255 per channel**, where plain alpha blending on the same pair differs by **57**. It is not byte-identical because the accumulation buffer is half-float and the framebuffer rounds after *each* blend — float addition is commutative, the rounding between two additions is not. The test measures the alpha-blended control **first** and refuses to run the claim unless that control differs by more than 16, because a scene where order does not matter would let the assertion pass against anything. A separate case pins that the near surface dominates, since an equal average is also order independent and is not transparency. `resolve` **discards** where nothing was accumulated, exactly, so the pass can sit in a chain unconditionally. 7 cases. |
| MOD-2107 | The weight function, written twice and compared | ✅ | The CPU twin takes the **same three arguments in the same units** and computes them in the same order as the GLSL, which is the whole point — the first version took a pre-normalised depth and would have been a second function that merely resembled the first. Compared across a 32-column depth ramp on the GPU. The comparison is done in **log space**: the weight spans four orders of magnitude, an 8-bit target cannot carry the value, and a linear scale would have tested only the clamp. |
| MOD-2108 | What OIT costs and what it gets wrong | ✅ | `cna_test_cnaext_transparency` and a new `CNAEXT_Transparency` gate; numbers in `docs/cnaext-perf.md`. **Order independence costs about 1.75× per surface and the ratio does not improve with count** — the marginal cost of one more surface is 0.098 ms sorted against 0.171 ms order-independent, which is what writing two render targets per fragment instead of one costs, a bandwidth ratio rather than an overhead that amortises. On top sits a fixed 1.3–1.8 ms of two clears and one resolve, half the difference at 8 surfaces and a sixth of it at 64. **The sort is not the cost**: 64 entries are sorted on the CPU inside the measured loop and the sorted path still wins by a factor that tracks fill rate. **And the approximation is measured, not merely described.** Sorted blending is *exact* for surfaces that do not interpenetrate, so it is a reference rather than an opinion: on eight surfaces spread across 2 to 60 units of depth, weighted blending differs from the exact frame by **35/255 on average and 142/255 at worst** over 40 708 covered pixels. That is a large deviation and it is the known failure mode rather than a defect — the weight is a function of depth alone, so a stack at very different depths reads flatter than true compositing. This scene is near the worst case; at similar depths, which is where sorting is hardest, the two agree closely. |
| MOD-2109 | Soft particles: fade `ParticleSystem` billboards against the prepass depth | ✅ | `setDepthInputEXT` and `setSoftnessEXT`, both off by default so a game that never asked keeps the hard edges it had. A billboard that intersects geometry shows a straight cut line where the two meet, and nothing else in a frame has an edge like it — it is the clearest tell that a smoke plume is a set of quads. Measured: with the wall touching the particles the frame carries **320**, with the wall well behind them **16320**, a 51× separation. **GPU draw path only, and the fallback's price is stated rather than hidden**: the CPU path builds billboards on the CPU and draws them through the stock effect, which has no depth input and no shader of its own to add one to. The test cost an investigation worth recording, because the code was right the whole time. Its first version stacked 64 particles on the same eight-by-eight patch of screen and read 45% where it expected zero — **alpha compounds**, and a 2% contribution repeated 64 times is `1 - 0.98^64`, which is 47% and looks exactly like a fade that is not working. Three probes were needed to establish that the shader's fade really was 0.02 and that the residual was the scene. The test now uses **one** particle, and its second version had another fault the probes caught: it moved the *particles* rather than the *wall*, so a nearer billboard covered more screen and the test was measuring perspective. Only the wall moves now. |
| MOD-2110 | Documentation: the transparency section, and how to choose between the two paths | ✅ | A section in `docs/cnaext-engine-layer.md` that opens by quoting what the layer used to say about itself, gives each path's failure direction in one line — few large surfaces at very different depths → `Sorted`, since the approximation is worst exactly there; many small overlapping ones or anything that interpenetrates → `OrderIndependent`, since that is where sorting is hardest — and prices the choice at 1.75× per surface. **The thing neither can do is named**: `PbrMaterialExtensions`' transmission samples the *opaque* frame, which by definition does not contain the transparent surfaces, so a transparent object seen through another is composited and never refracted. That is a boundary of both paths rather than a gap in one. Two rows added to the per-renderer matrix, and the sorted one is ✅ everywhere because ordering is plain arithmetic with no renderer in it. |

### 21.2 The shadow detail PCF cannot reach (`MOD-2120`–`MOD-2123`)

| ID | Task | Status | Acceptance criterion |
|---|---|---|---|
| MOD-2120 | `ContactShadowPass`: a short screen-space ray march in the prepass depth | ✅ | A `PostProcessPass` that reconstructs a view position from the prepass depth, walks a short ray toward the light and multiplies the pixel down where the depth image crosses it. The march happens in **view-space world units** — the reconstruction is scaled by the far plane before stepping — so `setMaxDistance` is a distance a level designer can reason about rather than a number of texels that means something different at every depth. The default is 25 cm, and the row that pins it is not decoration: a metre-long ray is a bad shadow map, noisier and more expensive and wrong wherever the occluder leaves the screen. Four requirements are checked before the march and each refusal **names itself** through `getFallbackReason` — no depth, no far plane, no inverse view, a degenerate direction — because the fallback is a frame with no contact shadows in it, which is indistinguishable from a frame nobody asked for them in. The march reuses SsrPass's two hard-won details: `cnaSnapToTexel`, because filtering a depth edge invents a surface at a distance nothing in the scene is at, and the `samplePosition.z >= 0` break, because projecting a point behind the eye produces a plausible screen position mirrored through the origin. Asserted over a synthetic contact — an object 15 cm in front of a wall — that the wall beside it darkens, the wall past the ray's reach does not, the object does not shadow itself, and shortening the ray makes the shadow *end sooner* rather than fade. 23 cases. |
| MOD-2121 | The march's own boundary: thickness, and what it invents | ✅ | `setThickness`, and it is not a quality dial with a right answer — it decides **which of two wrong answers** the pass gives. A depth image records where a surface is and nothing at all about how far back the object behind it goes, so a ray that has fallen 15 cm behind the stored surface is either inside a solid object or past a thin one, and the image cannot say which. Too small and the shadow disappears at the far end of every real occluder; too large and a railing shadows everything behind it as though it were a wall. Tested in both directions and on both sides of the boundary: in the predicate itself (a 15 cm gap is a hit at 30 cm thickness and not at 5 cm) and on the GPU over the same synthetic contact, where the wide setting darkens the wall to under half and the narrow one leaves it within 4/255 of untouched. The predicate is written **twice** — `isOccluded` in C++ and `cnaContactOccluded` in the GLSL that `getOcclusionTestGlsl` supplies to the shader — and the two are compared on the GPU across twelve cases straddling the bias, the thickness and both far sides. |
| MOD-2122 | Composition with the shadow map rather than instead of it | ✅ | `combineVisibility` states the rule — the two terms **multiply**, clamped — and the test states the alternative it rejects, because the rule is only interesting against the thing people actually do. Adding occlusions, `1 - ((1 - s) + (1 - c))`, sends a pixel both terms half-shadow to **0** rather than 0.25: over a real contact both terms fire together, so the shadow gains a black core with a visible edge where only one of them still reaches, and that edge is what gives screen-space shadows their reputation. A product cannot do it — zero is the floor and nothing multiplies below it. In the pass itself the composition is **free and unavoidable**: it multiplies into an image that already carries the shadow map's term, so the arithmetic is the same one whether or not anyone names it. Asserted both ways — a scene already black stays black to within 1/255, and the same contact over a half-lit wall removes the same *fraction* rather than the same amount. |
| MOD-2123 | Cost and documentation | ⬜ | Measured per sample count; the section says it is a *complement* to shadow maps, not a replacement. |

### 21.3 Grading and output (`MOD-2130`–`MOD-2133`)

| ID | Task | Status | Acceptance criterion |
|---|---|---|---|
| MOD-2130 | 3D LUT colour grading: load a `.cube` file into a `Texture3D` | ⬜ | The industry-standard grading exchange format, which `ColorGradePass`'s lift/gamma/gain cannot express; needs `GraphicsCapability::Texture3D` and refuses without it. |
| MOD-2131 | The tetrahedral-vs-trilinear interpolation choice, decided by measurement | ⬜ | Whichever is chosen, the other is named with the reason; a LUT read with the wrong interpolation shifts neutrals, which looks like a grading choice rather than a bug. |
| MOD-2132 | Debanding dither on the HDR→LDR step | ⬜ | A smooth dark gradient loses its visible bands; asserted by counting distinct values across a ramp, not by looking. |
| MOD-2133 | Documentation and cost | ⬜ | Where in the chain each belongs, and why dither must come **after** the transfer function rather than before. |

### 21.4 The atmosphere on geometry, not only on the sky (`MOD-2140`–`MOD-2142`)

| ID | Task | Status | Acceptance criterion |
|---|---|---|---|
| MOD-2140 | Aerial perspective: apply `AtmosphericSky`'s model along the view ray to *geometry* | ⬜ | A distant mountain takes the sky's colour with distance; today the sky is atmospheric and everything in front of it is not, which is the giveaway that the sky is a backdrop. |
| MOD-2141 | One model, not two | ⬜ | The scattering shared with `AtmosphericSky` through one emitted GLSL string, for the reason `MOD-2035` charged the layer for. |
| MOD-2142 | Its relationship to `HeightFogPass` and `VolumetricFogPass` | ⬜ | Documented so the three are not stacked by accident; each is a different physical claim about the same air. |

### 21.5 Seeing what the layer is doing (`MOD-2160`–`MOD-2165`)

Nothing in the engine layer draws a debug shape or reports a GPU time. Both gaps were felt directly
in Phase 20: `docs/cnaext-perf.md` is measured with a CPU wall clock around a read-back, and every
frustum, probe grid, cluster and light bound was verified by arithmetic because there is no way to
look at one.

| ID | Task | Status | Acceptance criterion |
|---|---|---|---|
| MOD-2160 | `DebugDraw`: lines, boxes, spheres, frustums, batched into one draw | ⬜ | A `BoundingFrustum` and a `BoundingBox` can be drawn without a game writing a vertex buffer; batched, because a debug helper that costs a draw call per line is one nobody leaves on. |
| MOD-2161 | Gizmos for the layer's own types: light bounds, probe grids, cluster slices, cascade splits | ⬜ | Each of Phase 20's invisible structures can be seen; this is the tool the phase wanted and did without. |
| MOD-2162 | Depth-tested and overlay modes | ⬜ | A gizmo behind geometry can be hidden or drawn through it, chosen per submission. |
| MOD-2163 | `GpuTimer`: timer queries around a pass | ⬜ | Real GPU time rather than CPU wall clock; needs `GL_EXT_disjoint_timer_query` on ES and reports unsupported without it, rather than returning a CPU number wearing a GPU name. |
| MOD-2164 | Per-pass timings through `RenderPipeline` | ⬜ | Every pass in the chain reports its own cost for a frame, retrieved a frame late so nothing stalls. |
| MOD-2165 | Re-measure `docs/cnaext-perf.md`'s post-process table with GPU timings | ⬜ | The existing numbers stay, labelled as CPU wall clock, beside the GPU ones; a table that silently changed method would make its own history meaningless. |

### 21.6 Refused against this profile, with reasons (`MOD-2190`–`MOD-2193`)

| ID | Task | Status | Acceptance criterion |
|---|---|---|---|
| MOD-2190 | Anything temporal — TAA, temporal AO/SSR denoising, temporal upscaling | ⛔ | **Restated as a class, because five items in this phase would otherwise each want it.** All of them need a reprojected history buffer and motion vectors for *every* pixel including transparent ones, and `MOD-2033` delivered per-object velocity as an opt-in obligation on the application rather than a guarantee. Already refused individually in `MOD-610` and `MOD-2098`; naming the class stops it being re-proposed one technique at a time. |
| MOD-2191 | Hardware occlusion culling | ⛔ | **A latency problem, not a rendering one.** `GraphicsCapability::OcclusionQuery` exists, but a query read in the frame that issued it is a stall, and read a frame late it needs a persistent identity per object across frames — which is scene management, and `OQ-6` put scene management outside this layer. Frustum culling plus LOD already removes the bulk; the rest is the application's to structure. |
| MOD-2192 | Virtual shadow maps | ⛔ | **Same missing hardware as `MOD-2099`.** Sparse/virtual textures need `ARB_sparse_texture`, absent from GL ES entirely, and the page-table pass that emulates them is the virtual-texturing subsystem that row already refused. Cascades remain the reachable answer. |
| MOD-2193 | Screen-space global illumination | ⛔ | **Refused as redundant against what this layer already has, not as unreachable.** SSGI is a screen-space ray march — the same machinery as `SsrPass` — and its diffuse answer is what `LightProbeVolumeEXT` already supplies with none of the screen-space failure modes (offscreen light missing, disocclusion, view dependence). It would add a third indirect-light path that disagrees with the other two. Revisit only if probes prove insufficient for a real scene. |

## Appendix A — Shared type sketches referenced by the tasks

```cpp
// MOD-20 / MOD-200
namespace CNA::Graphics {
    /** @brief Everything a post-process pass may need for one invocation. */
    struct PostProcessContext {
        Microsoft::Xna::Framework::Graphics::Texture2D*      source        = nullptr;
        Microsoft::Xna::Framework::Graphics::Texture2D*      sourceDepth   = nullptr;  // MOD-501
        Microsoft::Xna::Framework::Graphics::Texture2D*      sourceNormals = nullptr;  // MOD-501
        Microsoft::Xna::Framework::Graphics::RenderTarget2D* destination   = nullptr;  // null = backbuffer
        int   width  = 0;
        int   height = 0;
        const RenderPipelineSettings* settings = nullptr;
        float elapsedSeconds = 0.0f;
        // MOD-505 camera block (SSAO and any depth-based pass):
        Microsoft::Xna::Framework::Matrix projection{};
        Microsoft::Xna::Framework::Matrix inverseProjection{};
        float nearPlane = 0.0f;
        float farPlane  = 0.0f;
    };
}
```

## Open questions for the project owner

These are the decisions this plan cannot make alone. Each has a **default** that will be followed if
no answer is given, so nothing is blocked on them.

| # | Question | Default if unanswered |
|---|---|---|
| OQ-1 | **Order of work.** Start on the HDR spine (Phase 0 → 1 → 2 → 3 → 7, first visible result = a tonemapped HDR frame), or on shadows first (Phase 8), which is the more visible feature but has a longer tail? | HDR spine first — everything else depends on float targets. |
| OQ-2 | **Reference renderer.** EasyGL under `OPENGLES3`, per CNAEXT.md §6? | Yes, EasyGL/`OPENGLES3`. |
| OQ-3 | **`CNA_CNAEXT` default.** Stay OFF (CNAEXT.md §7), or flip ON once the layer is real so it is actually compiled in CI? | Stay OFF; add a CI/preset build with it ON (`MOD-2`). |
| OQ-4 | **IBL binding shape** (`MOD-1222`): should `PbrEffect` take a `CNA::Graphics::ImageBasedLightEXT` (XNA header including a CNA header), or three separate `Texture*` setters (no layering inversion)? | Three separate setters. |
| OQ-5 | **Settings accessor** (`MOD-729`): expose `RenderPipelineSettings` from `GraphicsDevice`, or only from `RenderPipeline`? | Only from `RenderPipeline`. |
| OQ-6 | **Scene-draw contract** (`MOD-860`): app-driven `begin`/`end` for the shadow and prepass passes, or a `renderScene` callback the pipeline invokes? | App-driven begin/end. |
| OQ-7 | **Per-renderer rollout ambition.** All 17 shader-capable renderers (Phase 16), or a smaller committed set (EasyGL, Vulkan, D3D11, WebGPU) with the rest opportunistic? | Reference + Vulkan + D3D11 committed; the rest opportunistic. |
| OQ-8 | **Build verification depth.** Is a full `cmake-build-cnaext` configure+build expected in every session (slow from a clean container), or is targeted compilation of the touched module acceptable? | Full build of the affected target, incremental after the first configure. |
| OQ-9 | **Commit granularity.** One commit per `MOD` task (CLAUDE.md's rule), even when that means many small commits? | One commit per task. |
| OQ-10 | **Task-count preference.** Is this 820-task granularity right, or should tasks be coarser (one per class rather than one per method/shader)? | Keep this granularity. |
